| **43** | ✅ **Cross-package `-I` CMake wiring for `rosidl_typesupport_tickle_c`** - "TickLE Plan"'s own priority list, item 4 (its own "lower urgency, build ergonomics" label) - the user explicitly chose to proceed now, accepting that this can only be verified through real CI round trips (no ROS 2 install in this tool's own local dev/test environment). `ros2_cli.py`'s own `-I`/`--include-dir` flag (added alongside Milestone 38's `Ros2Resolver`) was implemented but never actually invoked by the real CMake extension - this closes that gap in `rosidl_typesupport_tickle_c_generate_interfaces.cmake`. **Scope turned out considerably larger than "add one flag"**, confirmed by reading `ros2/rosidl` (`rosidl_generator_c_generate_interfaces.cmake`) and `ros2/rosidl_typesupport` (`rosidl_typesupport_c_generate_interfaces.cmake`) directly rather than guessing - three genuinely separate pieces, all needed together for a cross-package nested reference to actually compile and link: (1) a `-I <share-root>` per dependency package for `ros2_cli.py`'s own **text parsing** (`resolve.Ros2Resolver`'s existing fallback already handles the rest) - computed as `${<pkg>_DIR}/../..` (ament_cmake's universal `share/<pkg>/cmake/<pkg>Config.cmake` layout, the same fact `rosidl_find_package_idl.cmake` itself already relies on), deduplicated per distinct install prefix; (2) a **compile-time include path** so a dependent package's own generated adapter (`#include "Vector3.h"` - every TickLE-generated file uses a bare filename, never a ROS-style nested path) can find another package's own generated header - `target_include_directories(... PUBLIC $<BUILD_INTERFACE:...>/msg,srv $<INSTALL_INTERFACE:include/${PROJECT_NAME}/rosidl_typesupport_tickle_c/msg,srv>)`, paired with a new `install(DIRECTORY ...)` for those headers (this package's own generated headers were **never installed at all** before this milestone - harmless until now, since nothing outside the package itself ever needed to `#include` them); (3) a **real link dependency** on that dependency's own `rosidl_typesupport_tickle_c` library target, since `ros2_adapter.py`'s own nested-field conversion calls a sibling's `__to_tickle`/`__from_tickle`/`_encode`/`_decode` functions *directly* (not through a dispatch table the way `rosidl_typesupport_c`'s own cross-package case gets to sidestep entirely) - `target_link_libraries(... PUBLIC ${<pkg>_TARGETS__rosidl_typesupport_tickle_c})`, the identical variable-driven pattern both real upstream generators already use for their own cross-package case. **A genuinely pre-existing, separate bug found by this same research, not introduced by this milestone**: `rosidl_export_typesupport_targets()` was already being called (since Milestone 1), but the `${PROJECT_NAME}::<target>` ALIAS target that mechanism's own generated extras file requires to actually exist (`rosidl_cmake_export_typesupport_targets-extras.cmake.in`'s own `if(NOT TARGET "${_target}")` guard) was never declared - meaning cross-package linking could never have worked even before anything needed it (a silent CMake `WARNING`, not a fatal error, so nothing before this milestone would have surfaced it) - fixed by adding the missing `add_library(... ALIAS ...)`, the same declaration `rosidl_generator_c_generate_interfaces.cmake` already makes for its own target. A real, separate configure-time bug caught and fixed *during this same pass* (not by CI): the header-install step's own `if(EXISTS ...)` guard would always be false at CMake *configure* time (the directories in question don't exist until the real *build* runs the generator, long after configure) - fixed by tracking `_tickle_has_msg`/`_tickle_has_srv` as real booleans set while iterating the package's own actual interface files, not re-derived from the filesystem too early. The C++ extension (`rosidl_typesupport_tickle_cpp_generate_interfaces.cmake`) needs none of this - confirmed it never invokes `ros2_cli.py`/Python at all, delegating entirely to the C extension's own already-generated handles. **Not yet verified against anything real**: no existing package in the conformance suite has a genuinely cross-package (not same-package-sibling) nested reference to exercise this against at all - `test_msgs/Nested.msg`'s own `BasicTypes` reference (Milestone 40) is same-package. The `${<pkg>_DIR}/../..` share-root computation was checked in isolation with a standalone `cmake -P` script simulating two dependency packages' own `_DIR` variables (correctly resolved and deduplicated to one search root), and the full file re-checked with `cmake -P` for gross syntax errors, but neither proves the *real* ROS 2 install layout matches what was assumed, nor that the alias/link-target machinery actually resolves in a real colcon build - both need a genuine CI round trip, very possibly more than one (this is exactly the class of thing Milestone 1's own multi-round-trip history warned to expect). Real CI (`Check all`) confirmed green on the first round trip (no regression to the existing single-package case), but that alone wasn't proof the cross-package path itself worked - only that nothing broke. **A real cross-package test scenario was then built specifically to settle that** (the user's own explicit choice, over parking this at "implemented but unverified"): a new `rosidl_typesupport_tickle_c_tests_dep` package (one message, `Leaf.msg`) alongside the existing `rosidl_typesupport_tickle_c_tests`, whose own new `Branch.msg` nests `rosidl_typesupport_tickle_c_tests_dep/Leaf` - a genuinely cross-package field, unlike `test_msgs/Nested.msg`'s own same-package `BasicTypes` reference (Milestone 40), which never reaches any of this milestone's own machinery at all. A new `test_dispatch_nested.c` round-trips a real value (both fields) through the standard `get_message_typesupport_handle()` dispatch chain, added to `check-all.yml`'s own existing dispatch-chain step. **Took three real CI round trips to get green, exactly the class of iteration expected going in** - two genuine bugs found and fixed along the way: (1) `ament_export_include_directories()` and the `target_include_directories()`/`install(EXPORT ...)` target-based export mechanism (two *separate* CMake subsystems, confirmed by the two different failure shapes - a soft warning from one, a hard CMake Generate-step error from the other) both unconditionally listed **both** `msg` and `srv` include-directory exports regardless of whether either was actually generated - `rosidl_typesupport_tickle_c_tests_dep` (message-only, no `.srv` at all) hit both, immediately; (2) a real ROS 2 convention unrelated to anything TickLE-specific - `rosidl_generator_cpp`'s own generated C++ struct for `Branch` `#include`s `Leaf`'s own C++ struct header directly, so the dependency package needed the identical C++ generator set (`rosidl_generator_cpp`/`rosidl_typesupport_cpp`/`rosidl_typesupport_tickle_cpp`) the consuming package already had, not just the C ones. **Round 3 confirmed fully green, including the actual round-trip itself** (`rosidl_typesupport_tickle_c cross-package nested dispatch: PASS` in the real CI log, not just "the step exited 0") - the cross-package `-I`/include-path/link-library machinery genuinely works end to end for a real ROS 2 colcon build now, not just in theory. | 1, 38 | ✅ real CI confirmed the actual cross-package round-trip passes, not just that nothing regressed - two real bugs found and fixed getting there, both unrelated to the original design's own core reasoning |

| **44** | ✅ **Functional + performance comparison of `rmw_tickle` against `rmw_fastrtps_cpp`/`rmw_cyclonedds_cpp`** - "TickLE Plan"'s own follow-on after the conformance-grammar work (Milestones 38-42), done directly by "TickLE Plan" (observation/measurement, not development work, per the user's own call), tracked as a living document rather than inline here - see **`rmw_tickle/COMPARISON.MD`** for the full methodology and results, both halves now done. **Functional**: real `test_rmw_implementation` run against all three (FastDDS/CycloneDDS: pristine `lyrical`-branch local run, 16/16 executables each; `rmw_tickle`: its own real-CI `jazzy`-branch+patched-build number, 14/14) - individual `TEST_F()` case tallies and the full itemized skip lists are in `COMPARISON.MD`. **Performance**: a standalone script (`.github/scripts/compare_rmw_perf.sh` - deliberately *not* a CI workflow, the user's own call after an initial `rmw-perf.yml` integration attempt was started and backed out), run for real on a box that happened to already have the `tickle-perf` rig's own `lyrical`-distro `~/rmw_perf_ws` provisioning. Two real bugs found and fixed getting a trustworthy number, not assumed away: the script's own missing "continue past a failed test" guard (a real per-combo failure aborted it before the summary table ever printed); and, more substantively, `useBuiltinTransports=false` alone left FastDDS's own separate "Data Sharing" feature (a QoS-level same-host shortcut independent of transport selection) still creating live `/dev/shm` segments during a run - fixed by also setting `data_sharing kind=OFF` on the default writer/reader QoS profiles, confirmed clean on a second run (no new `/dev/shm` segments at all). Result, genuinely verified this time: `rmw_tickle` runs consistently ~1.3-1.6x higher latency than both DDS vendors across every topic/sync-mode combination measured, throughput indistinguishable across all three (dominated by the test's own fixed send rate). Also surfaced, not chased down here: an intermittent `rmw_tickle` async-mode crash (a real data-consistency assertion, not reproduced on a second run - see Milestone 45's own row for what came of it). **Standing process note, the user's own call (2026-09-20)**: `.github/scripts/compare_rmw_perf.sh` stays run-by-hand only, never CI - and *also* never run autonomously by "TickLE Plan" just because a performance-relevant change landed. When a change lands that looks performance-relevant, "TickLE Plan"'s own job is to say so and propose re-running the comparison, then wait for the user's own go-ahead - not to decide on its own that a re-measurement is warranted and just do it. `COMPARISON.MD`'s own numbers get updated only following that explicit approval, not automatically, and not on every compile. | 14, 15, 42 | ✅ both halves done, see `COMPARISON.MD` for the full numbers |

| **45** | 🔶 **Close (or at least narrow) the ~1.3-1.6x latency gap Milestone 44 found** - "TickLE Plan"'s own root-cause analysis of `COMPARISON.MD`'s own real numbers. **Status as of this row's own last edit (see the addendum paragraph immediately below, plus `COMPARISON.MD`'s own "Milestone 45's own allocation-pooling change" section, for the full story)**: item (1) - pool/reuse the scratch conversion buffers - has since been implemented (`403840a`) and measured (both by TickLE Dev and, independently, by "TickLE Plan") - a real, verified-correct change that stays in the codebase, but an honest **negative result**: no measurable, above-noise latency improvement across every clean run so far. Item (2) is confirmed real but small (~3% of publisher-thread time). Item (3) remains genuinely open - needs off-CPU/scheduling-latency tracing, not the cycle-based `perf record` already tried. **Ranked by confidence, each grounded in the actual current code, not guessed**: (1) *highest confidence, most directly fixable* - `rmw_publish()` (`rmw_tickle/rmw_tickle/src/rmw_publisher.c`) does a fresh `allocator.allocate()`/`deallocate()` pair for a scratch `to_tickle()` conversion buffer on **every single publish call**; the receive path is worse - `subscriber_callback()` (`rmw_subscription.c`) `zero_allocate()`s a fresh ROS-shaped buffer per arriving message, queues it, and `rmw_take_with_info()` later does a `memcpy()` *and* `deallocate()` on top of that - three heap operations round-tripping one message, on a hot path most mature pub/sub stacks long ago stopped allocating on at all. (2) *high confidence, structural* - `rmw_publish()` also unconditionally pays `tt_Node_interrupt()` (a real `eventfd` `write()` syscall, `hal_linux.c`) plus a full `pthread_mutex_lock()`/`unlock()` and a `tt_get_ns()` (`clock_gettime()`) on every call, regardless of whether the poll thread was actually blocked in a way that needed interrupting - correctness-motivated (the same `tt_Node_interrupt()`-then-lock contract every entry point here follows, `rmw_tickle.h`'s own doc comment), but a fixed per-call cost none the less. (3) *plausible, not measured in isolation* - delivery requires a real cross-thread hand-off: the poll thread decodes and queues a sample and broadcasts `context_impl->wait_cond`, but the application's own executor thread (blocked in `rmw_wait()`) has to actually be OS-scheduled awake before it can `rmw_take()` it - a real context-switch cost a tighter single-thread event-loop design wouldn't pay. (4) *context, not a bug* - TickLE/`rmw_tickle` has so far prioritized simplicity and predictable worst-case latency (Milestone 13's own "flush immediately by default, batching opt-in" choice) over the kind of multi-year micro-optimization FastDDS/CycloneDDS have both had - some of this gap is just that maturity difference, not something any single fix closes. **Proposed first target**: (1) above - pool/reuse the scratch conversion buffers (publisher-owned, subscriber-queue-entry-owned) instead of a fresh `malloc`/`free` per message, the most contained, least architecturally risky change of the four, and the one most directly supported by the profiling done so far (an actual counted allocation on the hot path, not a hypothesis). **Explicitly not scoped here, left to whoever picks this up**: whether (2)/(3) are worth pursuing at all given their cost (correctness-contract or architecture changes, not a contained local fix) versus (1)'s own likely return - a real profiling pass (not just counting allocations by reading the code) once (1) lands would tell whether the remaining gap is dominated by (2), (3), or something not yet identified at all. **Verification bar**: re-run `.github/scripts/compare_rmw_perf.sh` the same way Milestone 44 did (with the same FastDDS Data Sharing / CycloneDDS shared-memory forcing already verified working) and update `COMPARISON.MD`'s own numbers - the actual bar is a real, repeat, verified-clean measurement showing the gap narrowed, not just "the allocation is gone so it must be faster." **Separately tracked, not part of this milestone's own scope**: the intermittent `rmw_tickle` async-mode crash Milestone 44 also surfaced (`Data consistency violated. Received sample with not strictly higher id.`, `SIGABRT`, not reproduced on a second run) - a correctness bug, not a performance one, root-caused and documented separately, Milestone 47.

**(1) implemented and measured (2026-09-20) - a real, verified-correct change, but an honest negative result on the actual latency question.** `rmw_publish()` (`rmw_tickle/rmw_tickle/src/rmw_publisher.c`) gained a per-Publisher `publish_scratch_buf` (allocated once at `rmw_create_publisher()` time, sized `callbacks->tickle_struct_size`) plus a dedicated `publish_mutex` serializing concurrent `rmw_publish()` calls on the *same* Publisher around it (rmw's own "safe to call concurrently on the same publisher" contract, `rmw/rmw.h`) - safe to reuse immediately since `tt_Publisher_publish()` (verified by reading it, not assumed) never retains a pointer to its own `data` argument past the call itself, either encoding straight from it or `memcpy()`-ing the *encoded wire bytes* into `reliable_cache`. `rmw_subscription.c` gained a `shell_pool` (a small free-list of already-allocated "shell" buffers, sized `queue_capacity` - the most that can ever be genuinely in flight between `subscriber_callback()` writing one and `rmw_take()` draining it) that `subscriber_callback()`/`rmw_take_with_info()` pop/push instead of `zero_allocate()`/`deallocate()`. **The one real correctness hazard reuse introduces**: after `rmw_take_with_info()`'s own shallow `memcpy()` transfers a heap-owned field's pointer *value* out to the caller, the drained shell's own copy of that pointer is still sitting there unchanged - reusing it without zeroing first would make the next `from_tickle()` call free/reallocate memory the earlier caller still owns (a real use-after-free/double-free) - `shell_pool_push()`'s own `memset()` is what prevents it, confirmed (not just reasoned through) by a new dedicated regression test, `rmw_tickle/test/test_publish_take_reuse.c` (a `fake_ros_msg` with a heap-owned `char* text` field run through several reuse cycles on the same Subscription, asserting `shell_pool_count` directly to prove the pool is actually the mechanism at work) - passes clean under a full ASan+UBSan+leak-detection rebuild of the whole `rmw_tickle` test suite, not just a normal build. **The actual latency measurement**: re-running `.github/scripts/compare_rmw_perf.sh` twice (same box, same methodology as Milestone 44) shows **no measurable, above-noise improvement** - one topic/sync-mode combination even came in marginally higher than Milestone 44's own baseline, within the same run-to-run noise band the DDS vendors' own numbers show here too. See `COMPARISON.MD`'s own new "Follow-up (2026-09-20)" section for the full numbers and reasoning (most likely explanation: at this message size, a same-size high-frequency `malloc`/`free` pair is probably already served out of glibc's own `tcache` in tens of nanoseconds - genuinely below this measurement's own microsecond-scale noise floor). The change stays in the codebase regardless (fewer allocator calls is a real, if unmeasured-here, win on its own), but it does not close this milestone's own gap. Run 1 of the two measurement attempts hit Milestone 47's own already-root-caused, deliberately-deferred cross-instance crash on all four `rmw_tickle` combinations (unrelated to this change) - retried, matching Milestone 44's own identical recovery. **(2)/(3) still not attempted** - closing this gap for real now needs an actual profiling pass (`perf`/`ftrace`, not counting allocations by reading the code), tracked as the next real step.

**(2)/(3) real profiling pass (2026-09-20)** - a genuine `perf record -g` capture of both processes in a real two-process `rmw_tickle` `perf_test` run (`Array1k`, `ROS2` communicator, reverse-engineered CLI shape from `buildfarm_perf_tests`' own generated launch file), not a guess from reading the code. **Blocked, then unblocked, by the box's own `perf_event_paranoid=4`** - fully denied `perf` access for a non-root user; the user lowered it to `1` themselves via `sudo sysctl` in their own terminal (this tool has no interactive TTY for `sudo`). A fully-concurrent capture of both sides at once hit a second, narrower limit - `perf_event_mlock_kb=516` is too small for two simultaneous `perf record -g` ring buffers, so one side's capture (`pub.data`) came back empty while the other (`sub.data`, 10.1 MB, real samples) succeeded; worked around by re-running the publisher alone, profiled, against an unprofiled subscriber (`pub2.data`, 7.98 MB, 44578 samples) instead of raising `perf_event_mlock_kb` - each side's own internal call-graph doesn't depend on being captured from the same exact run as the other side, so `sub.data` + `pub2.data` together stand in for the originally-planned single simultaneous capture. **Candidate (2) - `rmw_publish()`'s own `tt_Node_interrupt()` + lock pair (`pub2.data`)**: real, but the hypothesis's two halves turn out to have very different weight. `rmw_publish()` itself accounts for 17.94% of all publisher-thread samples; within that, `tt_Node_interrupt()` (confirmed by reading `hal_linux.c`'s `tt_wake_signal()` - just an `eventfd` `write()`) alone is 3.09% - a real, non-trivial, unconditional-per-call cost, roughly a sixth of `rmw_publish()`'s own total. The lock half is the surprise: `tt_Publisher_publish()` (TickLE core) itself takes zero locks (confirmed by grep - no `mutex` symbol anywhere in its body), and `rmw_publish()`'s own two lock/unlock pairs (`publish_mutex`, `node_mutex`) are cheap in practice - only ~0.15% of samples land in `pthread_mutex_lock` with `rmw_publish` as the caller (the great majority of the profile's overall `pthread_mutex_lock`/`unlock` self-time, ~0.7%/0.6%, actually belongs to *other* callers entirely - `rclcpp::Publisher::do_inter_process_publish()`'s own internal locking and, separately, the poll thread's own `node_mutex` acquisition, neither part of the `rmw_publish()` call path). **Real conclusion for (2)**: the `eventfd` write is the genuine structural cost here, not the lock - an uncontended `pthread_mutex_lock`/`unlock` pair is fast enough to be nearly invisible next to it. **Candidate (3) - poll-thread-to-executor cross-thread hand-off (`sub.data`)**: `rmw_wait()`, `rclcpp::Executor::wait_for_work()`, and every `pthread_cond_wait`/`broadcast_wait_cond()`/`pthread_cond_broadcast` call site (`rmw_node.c`, `rmw_subscription.c`) combined account for under 0.02% of sampled time - by this measure, negligible. **But this is very likely a measurement-method artifact, not a real "it's free" finding, and is reported as such rather than overclaimed**: `perf record`'s default cycle-based sampling only ever samples a thread while it is actually running on a CPU - the executor thread's own wait for the hand-off is, by definition, spent blocked (off-CPU) in `pthread_cond_wait`, invisible to this kind of profile regardless of how long the real wall-clock wake-up latency actually is. What this capture *can* honestly say: the *on-CPU* cost of both sides of the hand-off (the notify call in the poll thread, the eventual resumption in the executor) is small enough to be under this profile's own detection floor across a 30-second, tens-of-thousands-of-samples run. What it *cannot* say: how many microseconds actually elapse between `broadcast_wait_cond()` firing and the executor thread resuming - that needs `perf sched record`/`perf sched latency` (off-CPU scheduling-latency tracing) or direct `tt_get_ns()` timestamping at both ends, neither of which was attempted this pass. **Net result for Milestone 45**: (2) is confirmed real and quantifiable (the `eventfd` write, ~3% of publisher-thread time, ~1/6 of `rmw_publish()`'s own cost) with the lock-pair portion of the original hypothesis now ruled out as the actual weight-bearing cost; (3) remains genuinely open - this pass's tooling was the wrong instrument to answer it, not evidence that the cost is zero. | 44, 45 | ⬜ (1) implemented, no improvement measured; (2) confirmed real (~3% of publisher time is the `eventfd` write, not locking) via real `perf` capture; (3) still unresolved - needs off-CPU/scheduling-latency tracing, not a cycle-based profile |
**(3) resolved with a real number (2026-09-21, TickLE Plan)** - the earlier `perf record` capture
above correctly identified it couldn't answer this (off-CPU time is invisible to cycle-based
sampling); `perf sched record`/`perf sched latency` (the suggested proper tool) needs kernel
tracepoint access this box doesn't have without an interactive `sudo` password this session has no
TTY for. Rather than ask for that (a system-wide change, not scoped to this one question),
measured the underlying primitive directly instead: a standalone userspace benchmark
(`/tmp/.../condvar_handoff_bench.c`, not committed - a throwaway measurement tool, not part of the
codebase) reproducing `rmw_tickle.h`'s own exact `wait_mutex`/`wait_cond` shape (`rmw_wait_set.c`'s
own `pthread_cond_wait()`/`pthread_cond_timedwait()` usage) - one thread holds the mutex, sets a
flag, `pthread_cond_broadcast()`s, releases; another thread is already blocked in
`pthread_cond_wait()` on the same pair. `t0` = `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` read immediately before the
broadcast; `t1` = read by the waiter immediately after `pthread_cond_wait()` returns (post-mutex-
reacquisition, exactly what `rmw_wait()` itself would observe). **Real result, reproduced 4/4 runs,
10000 samples each, on this same box**: avg ≈ 9.4-9.6μs, p50 ≈ 8.6-8.7μs, p99 ≈ 13-16.5μs, min ≈
2.7-4.3μs, a rare max outlier up to ~0.2-0.3ms (ordinary scheduler jitter, not a pattern). **This
lines up closely with the actual observed gap size**: `COMPARISON.MD` §5's own same-host numbers
put `rmw_tickle` at 46.4-54.3μs vs. FastDDS's 31.0-37.5μs and CycloneDDS's 32.2-35.7μs - a gap of
roughly 9-22μs, essentially the same magnitude as this benchmark's own typical (avg/p50) handoff
cost. **What this does and doesn't prove, stated plainly**: it shows the condvar handoff's own
*inherent* cost, isolated from every other per-call overhead, is the right order of magnitude to
explain the whole gap on its own - real, quantitative, reproducible evidence for item (3)'s own
standing hypothesis, not previously available. It does *not* prove no other DDS vendor pays a
comparable cost for their own equivalent internal hand-off (this benchmark has nothing to compare
against on that side - FastDDS/CycloneDDS's own receive-thread-to-user-callback mechanisms weren't
similarly isolated and measured this pass), so it's suggestive, converging evidence rather than a
closed proof that this specific mechanism is the *entire* gap. **Suggested next step for whoever
picks this up**: get the box's own `perf_event_paranoid` lowered (the user's own call, needs an
interactive `sudo` this tool can't run) to capture `perf sched latency` on a real two-process
`rmw_tickle` run directly, confirming this isolated number holds inside the real system too, not
just in a clean standalone reproduction.

| **46** | ✅ **Real RELIABLE implementation of `rmw_publisher_wait_for_all_acked()`** - Milestone 33's own explicitly-deferred item, done at the user's own direct request ("9순위 - `rmw_publisher_wait_for_all_acked()`의 RELIABLE 실제 구현"). Previously an honest `RMW_RET_UNSUPPORTED`: TickLE core's own ack bookkeeping ran the wrong direction for this - each reliable Subscriber tracked its own `ack_seq_no`, but a Publisher only ever reacted to ACKNACK, with no aggregated "which currently-matched peers have caught up" view at all. **TickLE core** (`include/tickle/tickle.h`/`src/tickle.c`): new `struct tt_Publisher.peer_ack_seq_no[tt_MAX_PEER_COUNT]`, index-aligned with `peers[]`, updated monotonically from `process_acknack()`'s own already-decoded cumulative `seq_no`; `forget_publisher_peer()` (replacing the generic `forget_peer()` call in `forget_peers_from_source()`'s own Publisher branch) resets a departed peer's slot in both arrays together, so a later, unrelated `node_id` reclaiming the same array index never inherits stale ack state. **The real design gap found mid-implementation**: a healthy, gap-free Subscriber never sends an ACKNACK at all (`maybe_arm_acknack_retry()`'s own deliberate "a healthy stream needs no ACKNACK" bandwidth optimization, unaffected even by a Heartbeat) - so aggregating only reactive ACKNACKs can never detect "fully caught up," the exact case this function most needs to prove. Real RTPS solves this with `HEARTBEAT`'s own `finalFlag` (clear = reply required regardless of gap state) - the same mechanism this milestone added: `struct tt_HeartbeatHeader` gains a `flags` field (`tt_HEARTBEAT_FLAG_FINAL`, the wire struct growing from 12 to 16 bytes to stay 4-byte aligned) and a new public `tt_Publisher_request_ack()` sends a solicited, `tt_HEARTBEAT_FLAG_FINAL`-clear Heartbeat straight to every currently-matched peer; `inform_subscriber_of_heartbeat()` (`tickle.c`) now replies with an ACKNACK even with no gap when that flag is clear. Every existing periodic/discovery-triggered Heartbeat still always sets the flag (unchanged behavior, verified via `tests/test_heartbeat.c`'s own updated `write_heartbeat()` helper now taking an explicit `flags` argument). **`rmw_tickle`** (`rmw_publisher.c`): `rmw_publisher_wait_for_all_acked()` now polls `peer_ack_seq_no[]` against the Publisher's own `seq_no` as of the call (a concurrent `rmw_publish()` mid-wait doesn't move the target), re-soliciting via `tt_Publisher_request_ack()` each `RMW_TICKLE_WAIT_FOR_ACKED_POLL_INTERVAL_NS` (reuses `acknack_retry()`'s own `tt_CALL_RETRY_INTERVAL` fallback cadence) - deliberately a bounded poll, not `context_impl->wait_cond`, since nothing broadcasts it on an ACKNACK specifically and holding `wait_mutex` across a nested `node_mutex` acquisition here would invert the lock order every other broadcast site (e.g. `check_publisher_deadline()`) already depends on (`node_mutex` outer, `wait_mutex` inner). `wait_timeout` `{0,0}` means poll-once/non-blocking, matching `rcl_publisher_wait_for_all_acked()`'s own explicit doc comment (`rcl/publisher.h`) one layer up. **Tests**: new cases in `tests/test_heartbeat.c` (`tt_HEARTBEAT_FLAG_FINAL` clear forces an ACKNACK with no gap / set stays silent, `tt_Publisher_request_ack()` unicasts to matched peers / no-ops with none / requires `reliable_cache`) and `tests/test_reliable_pubsub.c` (`process_acknack()` advances `peer_ack_seq_no[]` / never regresses it / ignores an unmatched sender, `forget_publisher_peer()` resets both arrays together); `rmw_tickle`'s own `test_rmw_api_surface.c` had a stale `RMW_RET_UNSUPPORTED` assertion replaced with the two cases a single-process test can actually exercise (nothing published yet, and something published but no wire-level peer - a same-node Subscription structurally never becomes one, `self_sent`-suppressed the same way DATA/ACKNACK already are). **A real, pre-existing bug found and fixed along the way, unrelated to this feature but blocking its own required CI gate**: `.github/scripts/compare_rmw_perf.sh` (Milestone 44) had been failing `Check all`'s shellcheck step on every push since it was added, unnoticed - a real `SC1011` parse error (an apostrophe inside a `:?` parameter-expansion message, misread as a single-quote terminator) plus unsuppressed `SC1090`/`SC1091` findings on its three `source` lines; fixed by rewording the message and adding `# shellcheck disable=` directives, matching `run_perf.sh`/`test.sh`'s own existing per-line convention. **Also flagged, not part of this milestone**: `send_heartbeat()`/`tt_Publisher_publish()`'s own unicast fast path assumes `peers[]` has no gap before the first `count_peers()` entries, which `forget_peer()`/`forget_publisher_peer()` alone don't guarantee (they null a departed peer's slot in place, not compact the array) - a real, pre-existing latent bug spotted while designing `peer_ack_seq_no[]`'s own index alignment, deliberately *not* relied on by `tt_Publisher_request_ack()` (which builds its own dense peer list instead) or fixed here; flagged as a separate follow-up task instead. **Proven green in real CI**: `make test`/`make sanitize` (ASan+UBSan)/`make -C platform/freertos`/`clang-format --dry-run --Werror`/`clang-tidy` (TickLE core) and `colcon build`/`colcon test`/`clang-tidy -p build/rmw_tickle` (`rmw_tickle`) all clean locally first; pushed, and both `Check all` and `Test all` confirmed green via `gh run watch` on the final commit (`32c0d0e`) - `Check all` needed a second round trip purely for the unrelated shellcheck fix above, `Test all` was green on the very first push. | 33 | ✅ real CI (`Check all`, `Test all`) confirmed green |
| **47** | ✅ **No per-Writer identity in the wire protocol - a real, confirmed cross-instance data-mixing gap, root-caused and (2026-09-20) implemented at the user's own direct request.** "TickLE Plan"'s own report: `rmw_tickle`'s async-mode two-process `buildfarm_perf_tests` benchmark (`.github/scripts/compare_rmw_perf.sh`, `Array1k`/`Struct16`, both) crashed once with `terminate called after throwing an instance of 'std::runtime_error' - Data consistency violated. Received sample with not strictly higher id. Received sample id 5 Prev. sample id: 4788` (`SIGABRT`, `performance_test`'s own receiver-side monotonicity check, `communicator.cpp`'s `update_lost_samples_counter()`) - did not reproduce on an immediate second run. **Root-caused, not just suspected**: 25 natural-repro iterations of the real two-process async test (both topics) did not reproduce it again (matches "did not reproduce" - this is genuinely rare/timing-dependent), but a clean, deterministic whitebox reproduction confirmed the exact mechanism directly against `tickle.c`'s own dispatch: `for_each_endpoint()` (`tickle.c`) matches incoming DATA purely by `(kind, endpoint_id)` - `endpoint_id = tt_hash_id(topic_name, endpoint_name)` - with **no check on `header->source` (the sending node's own identity) at all**. Fed one Subscriber a synthetic DATA stream from `node_id=50` reaching `seq_no=4788`, then one single DATA packet from a **different** `node_id=51` on the identical topic/endpoint name carrying `seq_no=5` - the callback fired for it unconditionally, reproducing `Received sample id 5 Prev. sample id: 4788` exactly. Combined with TickLE's fixed, shared UDP port (`_tt_NODE_PORT` 8282, `SO_REUSEADDR`+`SO_BROADCAST`, `hal_linux.c`) and no domain/session isolation, **any two independent TickLE processes on the same host publishing under the same topic+endpoint name are indistinguishable to a Subscriber** - not a RELIABLE-retransmission-ordering issue (`tt_MAX_RELIABLE_HISTORY` is only 64, far too small to explain a 4783-sample-old retransmission arriving this late) but a genuine identity-collision gap, most likely triggered by two test-harness processes (e.g. an old combo's slow-to-fully-exit publisher and a new combo's fresh one) briefly overlapping on the same host. **Confirmed this is a real DDS-parity gap, not "same topic name is itself wrong"** (the user's own correction, 2026-09-20): real DDS/RTPS intentionally allows multiple DataWriters on one topic - what makes it safe there is that every DataWriter/DataReader carries a GUID (`GuidPrefix` - unique per DomainParticipant, typically vendor+host+process+instance-derived - plus an `EntityId`), so a Reader tracks each matched Writer's own sequence/history/liveliness state *separately*, keyed by that GUID, never merging two Writers' streams into one implicitly-shared counter the way TickLE's flat `endpoint_id`-only matching does; DDS Writers/Readers also send an explicit "goodbye" (a dispose-flagged message, or participant lease expiry) on clean shutdown, promptly unmatching a departed Writer, narrowing the overlap window `real` vendors would need to hit the same failure shape (worth noting: `performance_test`'s own `update_lost_samples_counter()` check is itself writer-identity-blind - it just compares the payload-embedded id - so even real DDS could theoretically trip the identical assertion if two Writers' samples genuinely interleaved before an unmatch; DDS's GUID+goodbye combination just makes that window far narrower in practice than TickLE's, not structurally impossible). **Real fix - final design, decided across several rounds with the user (2026-09-20), still not implemented**: composite key `(node_id, entity_id)`, generalizing "Writer identity" to every entity kind TickLE has (`entity_id` covers what would be `pub_id`/`sub_id`/`server_id`/`client_id` depending on the entity's own `kind`) - `node_id` itself is *not* changed (stays the existing 1-byte, address-derived field, no discovery/wire change needed there at all; the original crash reproduced with two already-*different* `node_id`s, so `node_id`'s own known weaknesses were never actually the trigger). **`entity_id` generation**: a hybrid, not pure-random and not pure-linear, each chosen to cancel the other's own weakness (found by working through both alone first, in conversation): pure-linear-from-zero-per-entity alone would make a restarted process (same `node_id`, counter restarting at 0) very likely to *regenerate* an earlier run's own `entity_id` sequence, reproducing the identical collision the fix exists to prevent, just via restart instead of two-processes-overlapping; pure-random-per-entity alone works but reintroduces real per-entity collision math to reason about. Resolved as: one random base value drawn once per `tt_Node_create()` call (a per-launch "session" value, not per-entity), then each entity created on that node gets `entity_id = base + a simple per-node incrementing counter` (zero collision risk among entities coexisting within one run, and the random base makes two different *launches* of the same `node_id` occupy non-overlapping `entity_id` ranges with high probability - exact base width still open, 32 bits was the working assumption through this design discussion; wraparound arithmetic must be unsigned/well-defined, not a real concern at any realistic entity count either way). **Wire placement**: submessage-level headers (`tt_DataHeader`/`tt_AckNackHeader`/`tt_HeartbeatHeader`), *not* the shared message-level `tt_Header` - confirmed against DESIGN.md's own "RPC and Publish flush immediately by default; batching is opt-in" section that one datagram can legitimately batch submessages from *different* local entities sharing one `node_id`, so only `node_id` itself (already message-level, `tt_Header.source`) can safely stay there; `entity_id` needs its own field on each submessage type that identifies a specific local entity. **Explicit "goodbye" on clean shutdown**: folded into the *existing* discovery `UPDATE` announce mechanism (a flag/marker on it, not a new submessage type) rather than inventing a separate wire concept - fired from `tt_Publisher_destroy()`/`tt_Node_destroy()` et al. so a Subscriber can promptly drop that entity's own tracked state instead of waiting out `check_liveliness()`'s own timeout. With the random-base `entity_id` scheme above, goodbye is no longer the *only* thing preventing a restarted process from colliding with itself (the random base already makes that unlikely on its own) - its role narrows to promptness (freeing a Subscriber's own tracking-table slot right away) and to the residual crash-before-goodbye/before-liveliness-timeout window, which matches real DDS's own identical, accepted limitation (its own lease-expiry-based cleanup has the same gap for ungraceful shutdowns) - not a new weakness introduced here. **Subscriber-side data structure**: `struct tt_Subscriber`'s own single flat `ack_seq_no`/`received_bitmap`/`reliable_sender_node_id` fields become a small, fixed-capacity table of per-`(node_id, entity_id)` entries (a real WriterProxy equivalent, capped the same "no malloc after create" way every other fixed-size table in this codebase is) - `endpoint_id` (topic+entity name) still does first-level dispatch to *which* local Subscriber(s) via `for_each_endpoint()`, unchanged; `(node_id, entity_id)` is the new second-level key *within* that Subscriber's own now-plural tracking state. **Bonus, not the original goal but a real consequence**: generalizing to every entity kind also closes Milestone 35's own separately-documented, previously-accepted gap ("picking a specific one of several identically-named local endpoints is inherently ambiguous at the wire level" - `process_callrequest()`/`process_callresponse()`/`process_acknack()`'s own single-match `find_endpoint()` calls) - multiple same-named local Servers/Clients/Publishers become disambiguable the same way multiple same-named remote ones do, once every entity carries its own `entity_id` regardless of kind. Scope still explicitly open for whoever implements this: whether `entity_id` needs its own per-kind counter or one shared counter is fine (functionally equivalent, `kind` is already a separate dimension in existing matching - one shared `tt_Node`-level counter is the simpler implementation); the exact `entity_id` width. A substantially bigger wire-format change than Milestone 46's own `tt_HEARTBEAT_FLAG_FINAL` addition (touches multiple submessage header types, plus the discovery `UPDATE` format for goodbye, plus a real Subscriber-side data structure change) - this design pass narrows *what* needs building, not the size of building it.

**Implemented (2026-09-20), at the user's own direct request ("진행해줘") after TickLE Plan relayed the finalized design and reprioritized it ahead of further Milestone 45 work - built exactly to the design above, not redesigned.** `include/tickle/tickle.h`/`src/tickle.c`: `struct tt_Node` gains `entity_id_base`/`next_entity_id` (base drawn from `tt_get_ns()`'s own low 32 bits at `tt_Node_create()` time - no new RNG primitive; counter starts at 0); `struct tt_Endpoint` gains `entity_id`, assigned once in `add_endpoint_to_node()` (the single shared registration point for all four entity kinds) as `entity_id_base + next_entity_id++` - one shared counter, not per-kind, the simpler of the two explicitly-left-open options. `tt_DataHeader`/`tt_HeartbeatHeader` gain `entity_id` carrying the *sending* Publisher's own identity (both are Publisher-emitted, fanned out via `for_each_endpoint()` to every local match - no single "target" to name instead); `tt_AckNackHeader` gains `entity_id` carrying the *target* Publisher's own identity instead (mirroring that header's own existing `endpoint_id` "target Publisher" convention, populated from whichever `struct tt_WriterProxy` the ACKNACK answers) - a design detail not spelled out in the finalized text above but the only reading consistent with `tt_AckNackHeader.endpoint_id`'s own existing semantics. `tt_VERSION` bumped 2 → 3 (no partial-compatibility parsing needed, same reasoning as the 1 → 2 bump). New `struct tt_WriterProxy` (`node_id`, `entity_id`, `sender_ip`/`sender_port`, `ack_seq_no`, `received_bitmap`, `retry`, `acknack_scheduled`, `heartbeat_last_seq_no`, and a `sub` back-pointer - the same `{owner, self}` pattern `struct server_cache_clean_config` already uses, needed so a per-writer `acknack_retry()` schedule entry, now armed against `&writers[i]` instead of the whole Subscriber so independent writers' retry timers never collide, can still reach `sub->node`/`sub->endpoint`) replaces `struct tt_Subscriber`'s own single flat `ack_seq_no`/`received_bitmap`/`reliable_sender_*`/`reliable_retry`/`reliable_acknack_scheduled`/`reliable_heartbeat_last_seq_no` fields with a fixed `writers[tt_MAX_PEER_COUNT]` table; `find_writer_proxy()`/`find_or_create_writer_proxy()` (find-or-lazily-claim by `(node_id, entity_id)`, silently capped the same way `upsert_peer()` already is) are the new lookup primitives every rewritten function (`send_acknack()`, `acknack_retry()`, `advance_ack_seq_no()`, `skip_unrecoverable_backlog()`, `maybe_arm_acknack_retry()`, `jump_ack_baseline()`, `update_reliable_ack()`, `inform_subscriber_of_heartbeat()`) now operates through instead of the Subscriber's own former flat fields directly. **"Goodbye"**: rather than a new wire flag, implemented as *timing* - `tt_Node_destroy()` already broadcast a final entity-less UPDATE immediately (pre-existing); the same eager `build_and_send_update()`+`flush_tx()` pair (not `node_update()` itself, which would also re-arm its own periodic reschedule on top of the one already pending - safe inside `tt_Node_destroy()` only because that function wipes the whole scheduler right after) is now called from `tt_Publisher_destroy()`/`tt_Subscriber_destroy()`/`tt_Client_destroy()`/`tt_Server_destroy()` too, so destroying *one* entity while the node keeps running announces the reduced list right away instead of waiting up to `tt_NODE_UPDATE_INTERVAL` for the next periodic tick - `decode_update_entities()`'s own existing "authoritative announce" reconciliation (`forget_peers_from_source()`) does the rest on the receiving side, unchanged. **A real, honest scoping decision made during implementation, not in the original design text**: `struct tt_Peer`/`upsert_peer()`/`pub->peers[]` (and `tt_Client.peers[]`) stay keyed by `node_id` alone, *not* extended to `(node_id, entity_id)` - doing so would need `entity_id` added to the discovery `UPDATE` wire format too (`struct tt_UpdateEntity`), a fourth wire struct beyond the three the finalized design explicitly named; `tt_Publisher.peer_ack_seq_no[]`'s own existing "one Subscriber per remote node" simplification is untouched, a smaller-scope mirror of the exact same kind of limitation this milestone fixes on the *Subscriber* side, deliberately left as a separate, explicitly-flagged follow-up rather than silently expanding this milestone's own footprint. RPC's `tt_CallRequestHeader`/`tt_CallResponseHeader` generalization (the design's own "bonus... not the original goal") was likewise **not** implemented - out of scope by the design's own words. **A bonus that *was* implemented**, since it fell out of `entity_id` existing at zero extra wire cost: `find_endpoint_by_entity()` (mirrors `find_endpoint()`, prefers an exact `entity_id` match among several local Publishers sharing `endpoint_id`, falls back to the old first-match behavior when `entity_id` is 0/unknown) now backs `process_acknack()`'s own local-Publisher lookup, closing Milestone 35's own previously-accepted ACKNACK-routing ambiguity for real. **A second, real latent bug found and fixed while implementing this, not part of the original incident**: `rmw_tickle`'s own `rmw_get_gid_for_publisher()` (`rmw_publisher.c`) built each publisher's `rmw_gid_t` from `(node_id, endpoint_id)` under a doc comment explicitly (and, it turns out, incorrectly) claiming that pair was "already unique" - exactly the same name-hash-collision gap this whole milestone exists to fix, just surfaced at the rmw layer instead of the wire layer; switched to `(node_id, entity_id)` instead, closing it. **Honest, explicitly-acknowledged residual, matching the design's own text**: `deliver_data_to_subscriber()`'s own callback delivery stays unconditional regardless of writer identity, same as real DDS (which also delivers every matched Writer's samples without deduplicating by GUID) - this milestone's `entity_id`+WriterProxy table make RELIABLE ack-tracking correct when multiple writers *legitimately* coexist, and "goodbye" narrows the window a *departed* writer's stale traffic could still be confused with a newly-arrived one's, but neither mechanism makes TickLE reject a still-in-flight stale packet outright; the reported crash's own root cause (no writer identity at all) is fixed, its probability sharply reduced by prompt goodbye, but the theoretical residual race is the same one real DDS's own lease-expiry-based cleanup also has for an *ungraceful* shutdown - not a new weakness this milestone introduces. **Tests**: new `tests/test_writer_identity.c` (three cases reproducing the literal incident shape - a writer already deep into a stream, seq_no scaled down from the real 4788, unaffected by a second writer's low seq_no whether that writer differs by `node_id` or only by `entity_id` on the same `node_id`; independent per-writer gap/retry state) and a new `tests/test_publish_subscribe.c` case (`tt_Publisher_destroy()` on one of two Publishers broadcasts immediately, as a real broadcast, not just batched, while the surviving Publisher stays correctly registered); four existing test files (`test_heartbeat.c`, `test_reliable_pubsub.c`, `test_rxo_matching.c`, `test_durability_pubsub.c`) updated to construct/inspect `struct tt_WriterProxy` entries instead of the now-removed flat Subscriber fields. **Verified clean locally**: `make test`/`make sanitize` (ASan+UBSan)/`make -C platform/freertos`/`clang-format --dry-run --Werror`/`clang-tidy` (TickLE core) and `colcon build`/`colcon test` (10/10 passed)/`clang-tidy -p build/rmw_tickle` (`rmw_tickle`, the one touched file, `rmw_publisher.c`).

**A real bug the first push's own CI (`Test all`) caught, not a flaky failure**: `Illegal submessage length: 1472 < 4 || 1472 > 1468` in "Integration - Linux HAL over netns" - `tools/typesupport`'s own Python-side mirror of the wire format (`model.py`'s `FRAMING_OVERHEAD`, "DATA's framing is 24 bytes") was never updated for `tt_DataHeader`'s own 16 → 20 byte growth above, so `examples/perf/Bulk.msg`'s auto-derived array capacity (`examples/perf/Bulk.c`, generated) stayed 4 bytes too large for one datagram - the exact amount `tt_DataHeader` grew by. "Check all"'s own `make regen`-and-diff drift check didn't catch this: fresh generation and the committed output were both stale in the same way, so they still matched each other. Fixed: `FRAMING_OVERHEAD` 24 → 28 (`tools/typesupport/tickle_typesupport/model.py`), `DESIGN.md`'s own "Interface serialization" section corrected to match, `examples/perf/Bulk.c`/`.h` and `tools/typesupport/tests/golden/Bulk.c`/`.h` regenerated (payload capacity 1442 → 1438), `tests/test_roundtrip.py`'s own `BULK_PAYLOAD_CAPACITY` updated - full local pytest suite back to 66/66. Verified directly (a max-capacity `BulkData_encode_size()` now totals exactly 1472 bytes = `tt_MAX_BUFFER_LENGTH`) and via `make test-freertos`'s own real QEMU perf pair (identical `Bulk.c`, a real full-Ethernet-frame throughput run) passing end-to-end - the Linux netns equivalent needs `sudo` (not available in this sandbox), but exercises the same HAL-independent encode/decode path.

**Proven green in real CI on the corrected push**: `Test all`, `Check all`, `Performance Test`, and `rmw_tickle performance` all confirmed green via `gh run watch` (commits `12f18c6` then `3616f07`) - no further round trips needed. | none (a protocol-level design item, not built on any single prior milestone) | ✅ implemented exactly to the finalized design, real CI (`Test all`, `Check all`) confirmed green |

The to-do list itself surfaces what was previously scattered across the old document's own prose: the two real TickLE-core findings from the scenario 3-9 HIL work (a liveliness false-positive causing duplicate DURABLE delivery, and a RELIABLE+VOLATILE semantic difference from DDS needing a design decision), Milestone 45's own still-open items (2)/(3), and the still-outstanding cross-host `rmw_tickle` measurement gap. | **59** | ✅ **Liveliness false-positive causing duplicate DURABLE backlog delivery - fixed, first of the two scenario-3-9 findings Milestone 58's own to-do list surfaced.** TickLE Plan's own real reproduction (`durability_late_join` HIL scenario): a 20-sample backlog delivered 7 times over (140 total), correlated with "Node presumed dead" warnings under load. **Root cause**: `check_liveliness()`'s own presumed-dead cleanup (`tt_LIVELINESS_MISS_THRESHOLD` * `tt_NODE_UPDATE_INTERVAL` = 3s with no UPDATE heard - a load-induced gap in a still-alive peer's periodic announce, not necessarily a real departure) wipes `update_seen[]`/`update_last_modified[]`/`peers[]` for that source. This defeats `process_update()`'s own existing "nothing changed" dedup (`update_seen[source] && update_last_modified[source] == last_modified`, which only short-circuits when `update_seen` is still true) on the very next, otherwise-unchanged announce from the same still-running peer - `upsert_peer()` then sees a previously-empty slot again and looks like a genuinely new match, re-triggering `deliver_durability_backlog()` from `register_subscriber_peer_on_publisher()`. **Fix**: new `struct tt_DurableDeliveryRecord` (`node_id`, `last_modified`) and a `durable_delivered[tt_MAX_PEER_COUNT]` table added to `struct tt_ReliableCache` (`include/tickle/tickle.h`) - keyed by `(node_id, last_modified)`, not `node_id` alone, since `last_modified` (`tt_Node.last_modified`, a `tt_get_ns()` reading refreshed whenever that node's own Publisher/Subscriber/Client/Server set changes) is what actually distinguishes a genuine process restart (a real new match needing the backlog again) from the same continuous instance recovering from a transient liveliness gap (unchanged `last_modified`, already has the backlog). `check_liveliness()` deliberately never touches `durable_delivered[]`, so the record survives the presumed-dead wipe. New `durable_delivered_get()`/`durable_delivered_upsert()` helpers (`src/tickle.c`, mirror `upsert_peer()`'s own linear-scan style); `announce_last_modified` threaded through `struct update_peer_ctx`/`decode_update_entities()` from `process_update()`'s own already-decoded `last_modified`; `register_subscriber_peer_on_publisher()` now checks the table before calling `deliver_durability_backlog()` and records the delivery afterward - `send_initial_heartbeat()` (Milestone unnumbered, already shipped) is unaffected, still fires on every genuinely-new-slot match regardless. A full table-overflow (`durable_delivered_upsert()` finding no slot) is a safe no-op, same trade-off `upsert_peer()`'s own full-table case already makes - costs one redundant re-delivery, never a correctness problem. **Tests**: two new cases in `tests/test_durability_pubsub.c` - `test_durability_no_redelivery_after_liveliness_false_positive()` (simulates `check_liveliness()`'s real cleanup directly, then a same-`last_modified` re-announce - confirmed reproducing the exact bug before the fix, 0 redundant sends after) and `test_durability_redelivers_after_genuine_restart()` (the mirror case - a different `last_modified` after the same cleanup still gets the backlog, proving the fix doesn't just suppress delivery outright). Verified locally: `make test`/`make sanitize` (ASan+UBSan)/`make -C platform/freertos`/`clang-format --dry-run --Werror`/`clang-tidy` (bare invocation, both files) all clean. Task 2 of this same to-do list item (RELIABLE+VOLATILE semantic fix to match DDS) not yet started - a separate design task needing the user's own decisions along the way. | 58 | ✅ implemented, locally verified, and confirmed green on real CI (`e7eee0b`: `Test all`/`Check all`/`Performance Test`/`rmw_tickle performance` all `success` via `gh run view --json conclusion`) |

| **60** | ✅ **RELIABLE+VOLATILE DDS-parity fix and receive-side de-duplication - the second scenario-3-9 finding plus a third, separately-discovered item, both aligned to real DDS design at the user's own explicit request ("Task 2에 대해 DDS가 동작하는 방식에 맞추는 것이 좋을 것 같아... 2번 새 항목도 DDS와 설계를 맞추도록 하자").** Two distinct, real bugs found and fixed together since both live in `update_reliable_ack()`: **(1) RELIABLE+VOLATILE historical-backlog leak** (TickLE Plan's own original finding: 57 samples received where DDS RELIABLE+VOLATILE would give 0). Root cause: `find_or_create_writer_proxy()` hardcodes a fresh WriterProxy's `ack_seq_no = 1`; `inform_subscriber_of_heartbeat()` already special-cased first contact correctly (`ack_seq_no = ctx->first_available_seq_no`, its own doc comment calling this out as "the actual DDS-parity fix this whole follow-up is for"), but `update_reliable_ack()`'s DATA-arrival path never did - it discarded `find_or_create_writer_proxy()`'s own `first_contact` out-param entirely (passed `NULL`). Since `send_initial_heartbeat()` is a single unicast Heartbeat never itself retried/acked, DATA can legally win the race and arrive at a fresh Subscriber first, especially under loss injection (exactly `history_depth_burst_loss`/`lifespan_expiry`'s own scenario shape) - when it does, and the offset from the stale `ack_seq_no==1` default is small (< `tt_RELIABLE_BITMAP_BITS`), the gap logic misread "everything before this first sample" as a recoverable in-flight loss and ACKNACK-requested a VOLATILE Publisher's own pre-match history it was never obligated to keep. **Fix**: `update_reliable_ack()` now reads `first_contact` and, on first contact, syncs `proxy->ack_seq_no = seq_no` before the existing exact-match/gap comparisons run - real DDS readers have zero basis to assume anything existed before the very first sample they ever see for a writer, absent an explicit Heartbeat saying otherwise. Requires no new wire field or discovery dependency: for a DURABLE Publisher, the very first DATA a fresh Subscriber sees is `deliver_durability_backlog()`'s own oldest-retained sample anyway (not necessarily 1, matching TRANSIENT_LOCAL's own real "current retained depth" semantics), so this generalizes correctly for both durability modes without knowing the remote Publisher's own durable flag at all. `process_acknack()` itself is deliberately untouched - gating it by `pub->durable` would have also broken the legitimate in-flight-loss recovery RELIABLE-without-durability still guarantees for an *already-matched* Subscriber; the fix belongs entirely on the Subscriber's own baseline-sync side. Accepted, narrow residual (same category as Milestone 47's own honest residuals): a reordering-at-first-contact edge case (an earlier backlog sample lost in flight while a later one wins the race) is only caught correctly when the Heartbeat happens to win that race instead, unaffected by this branch. **(2) Receive-side de-duplication** (TickLE Plan's own separate real HIL finding: `history_depth_burst_loss`/`lifespan_expiry` `recv > sent`, uncorrelated with any "presumed dead" warning, ruling out Milestone 59's own liveliness family as the cause). Root cause: `deliver_data_to_subscriber()` invoked the application callback unconditionally on every DATA arrival, with no seq_no-based gate at all - real DDS readers de-duplicate by (writer GUID, sequence number) before ever notifying the listener, but TickLE had no equivalent, so any retransmission that overlapped with an already-received sample (a legitimate ACKNACK-driven repair racing the original, or a stray duplicate) double-delivered to the app. **Fix (first attempt, reverted after a real-CI-caught regression - see below)**: `update_reliable_ack()` now returns `bool` (`true` = genuinely new, `false` = already accounted for) instead of `void` - the out-of-order bitmap branch checks whether the bit was *already* set before OR-ing it in, returning `false` if so; `deliver_data_to_subscriber()` skips the callback entirely when `false`. A best-effort Subscriber (`!sub->reliable`) always returns `true` - no per-writer tracking to de-duplicate against and no retransmission mechanism to ever legitimately produce a duplicate in the first place, matching real DDS BEST_EFFORT's own identical non-guarantee.

**A real, severe regression the first push's own CI (`Performance Test`) caught, not a flaky failure**: the first attempt above *also* flipped the existing `seq_no < proxy->ack_seq_no` no-op branch to return `false` (treating anything below the cumulative watermark as an always-duplicate) - `github-action-benchmark`'s own `fail-on-alert` correctly failed the push: **"reliable recv throughput" collapsed from a consistent ~820 Mbps (7 straight prior pushes on this exact rig, `Reliable throughput (perf_client/perf_server -R)` benchmark history) to 66.276 Mbps, a 12.38x drop**, while the same run's own best-effort throughput stayed a normal ~900 Mbps - ruling out rig/network noise. **Root-caused, not guessed**: `examples/linux/perf/perf_server.c`'s own `total_received_msgs` (incremented once per `bulk_callback()` invocation) was capped at exactly 65,536 for the whole run - `jump_ack_baseline()` (fired when a gap exceeds `tt_RELIABLE_BITMAP_BITS`, plausible under real reordering at near-line-rate on real hardware, not just genuine loss) *abandons* tracking for its own jumped-past range rather than confirming any of it was actually received, unlike the ordinary exact-match/bitmap-absorption path where advancing past a position always means it genuinely arrived - every still-in-flight (merely reordered, never lost) sample from that abandoned range then legitimately arrives with `seq_no < ack_seq_no` and was being wrongly treated as an already-delivered duplicate, silently dropping the vast majority of a saturated stream's own genuinely-new samples from ever reaching the application callback. **Corrected fix**: narrowed the dedup gate to *only* the bitmap-bit-already-set case - `seq_no < proxy->ack_seq_no` reverted to `return true` (deliver, matching pre-milestone behavior for this branch exactly) - since a set bit is always fresh, current-window evidence (`jump_ack_baseline()` itself zeroes `received_bitmap` on every jump, so a set bit can never be contaminated by an abandoned range), while a bare watermark comparison alone is not reliable once a jump has ever happened. This narrower gate no longer catches an already-cumulatively-passed-watermark duplicate (an accepted, narrow miss, same category as this file's other honest residuals) but carries no risk of misclassifying a genuinely new sample. **Tests**: `tests/test_reliable_pubsub.c` gained `test_reliable_first_contact_via_data_does_not_request_pre_match_history()` (seq_no 50 first contact, offset 49 - confirmed reproducing the exact leak before fix #1: `ack_seq_no` stuck at 1, a real ACKNACK sent requesting 1..49; after the fix, `ack_seq_no` syncs to 51, zero ACKNACKs, and a later genuine gap still tracks normally), `test_reliable_duplicate_delivery_is_not_re_delivered_to_callback()` (the one safely-dedupeable duplicate shape - an out-of-order seq_no arriving twice before the watermark reaches it - plus an explicit assertion that a below-watermark duplicate is deliberately *not* suppressed, documenting the accepted trade-off directly in the test), and `test_reliable_reordered_arrivals_after_baseline_jump_are_still_delivered()` (the exact real-CI regression shape, pinned permanently: a gap wide enough to trigger `jump_ack_baseline()`, then several reordered - never lost - arrivals from the abandoned range, confirmed reproducing the silent-drop before the corrected fix, still delivered after). One pre-existing test required a conscious update, not a silent behavior change absorbed: `tests/test_writer_identity.c`'s own `test_gap_on_one_writer_does_not_affect_another()` used to open its test gap via literal first contact at seq_no 2 (implicitly relying on the exact leak fix #1 closes) - changed to open the gap on an already-established writer instead (first contact at seq_no 1, then a real gap at seq_no 3), preserving the test's own actual purpose (independent per-writer retry state) without depending on the now-corrected ambiguous behavior. Every other pre-existing test either had first contact exactly at seq_no 1 (no behavior change from fix #1) or already exercised the `offset >= tt_RELIABLE_BITMAP_BITS` jump path (`test_reliable_subscribe_oversized_first_gap_jumps_baseline_instead_of_freezing`, `test_durability_backlog_recovered_via_acknack_when_reliable_too` - both produce numerically identical results under the new first-contact sync, confirmed by inspection and by the full suite passing unchanged). Verified locally, both before and after the correction: `make test`/`make sanitize` (ASan+UBSan)/`make -C platform/freertos`/`clang-format --dry-run --Werror`/`clang-tidy` (bare invocation, all touched files) all clean; every new test independently confirmed to fail with its exact reproduced symptom when the relevant fix is reverted, then pass again once restored. Both pushes (`dab6f49` then `ad6d48b`) fully confirmed green on real CI, `Performance Test` included (`reliable recv throughput` recovered to 822.987 Mbps, matching the pre-regression ~820 Mbps baseline exactly).

**(3) The remaining real regression TickLE Plan's own re-verification found - fix #1's own leak, unaffected by fix #1 at all.** Re-running `history_depth_burst_loss`/`lifespan_expiry`/`durability_late_join` on real HIL after both pushes above: `lifespan_expiry` improved (recv ≤ sent, real loss only, no more duplicate signal); `history_depth_burst_loss` unchanged at the time (still `recv > sent`) - **initially misdiagnosed here as consistent with (2)'s own accepted residual (a duplicate arriving after a normal, non-jump watermark advance); actually the same root cause as (3) below, confirmed once the fix below landed and this scenario's own `recv > sent` signal disappeared entirely (replaced by ordinary `recv < sent`, real loss only) - `history_depth_burst_loss/server.c`'s own Subscriber is `sub.reliable = true` but never sets `sub.durable` (volatile, the same shape as `durability_late_join`), deliberately joins late (`pause_s`, default 3.0s) while the Publisher keeps a `depth = 8` `reliable_cache` (`client.c`), so the exact same Heartbeat-driven backlog leak this fix closes was inflating this scenario's own `recv` count too, not (2)'s own bitmap-duplicate case - corrected once actually re-verified, not left as the original wrong call**; but `durability_late_join`'s own VOLATILE case was **completely unaffected by fixes #1/#2** - still deterministically 57 (later re-measured as) samples received, decimal-identical to before fix #1. **Root-caused**: that scenario's server publishes its whole backlog *before any Subscriber exists* (`examples/perf_hil/tickle/durability_late_join/server.c`'s own doc comment - "the actual thing under test"), with `pub.reliable_cache` depth 64 comfortably above the 20-sample backlog, so nothing is ever evicted. For a VOLATILE (`-D` omitted) Publisher, `deliver_durability_backlog()` correctly no-ops, but `send_initial_heartbeat()` fires regardless (gated only on `pub->reliable`, never `pub->durable`) and announces `first_available_seq_no = reliable_cache_oldest_seq_no()` - the oldest sample *still retained*, which is `1` here since nothing evicted - and `inform_subscriber_of_heartbeat()`'s own pre-existing first-contact branch (Milestone unnumbered, already in the codebase before this milestone) unconditionally synced `ack_seq_no` to that value, regardless of the Subscriber's own requested durability. Since the discovery-triggered Heartbeat reaches a fresh Subscriber before any live DATA in this exact "backlog written before match" shape, `update_reliable_ack()`'s own fix #1 (the DATA-arrival first-contact sync) never even runs - the Heartbeat path resolves first contact on its own, unaffected by anything fix #1 changed. **Analyzed against real DDS/RTPS semantics before implementing, at the user's own explicit request** ("DDS가 어떻게 동작하는지 먼저 분석해줘... 승락을 받고 진행"): DURABILITY is one of DDS's Requested-vs-Offered (RxO) QoS policies, the same family as RELIABILITY/DEADLINE/LIVELINESS/etc. - for every RxO policy, the *Offered* side (Publisher) only gates compatibility (`offered >= requested`, already enforced by `subscriber_incompatible_with_publisher()`), while the *Requested* side (Subscriber) defines what that Subscriber actually gets out of the match. A VOLATILE-requesting Subscriber explicitly does not want pre-match history, even matched against a Publisher that happens to be durable and could offer it (a legal, common DDS pattern - e.g. a monitoring reader that only wants live updates) - real RTPS reflects this by initializing a newly-matched VOLATILE reader's own tracked baseline to the writer's *current* position (`lastChangeSequenceNumber` at match time), not its oldest retained sample; a TRANSIENT_LOCAL-or-better reader gets the latter. **Fix, keyed on `sub->durable` (the Subscriber's own already-existing local field, no new wire data or discovery dependency needed) rather than `pub->durable`**: `inform_subscriber_of_heartbeat()`'s first-contact branch now syncs `ack_seq_no` to `ctx->first_available_seq_no` only when `sub->durable`; otherwise to `ctx->last_seq_no + 1` (not `last_seq_no` itself - `ack_seq_no` means "next NOT YET accounted for" per `struct tt_WriterProxy`'s own doc comment, so leaving it at `last_seq_no` would still treat that one already-published sample as outstanding and trigger a real, if narrow, off-by-one ACKNACK - caught by inspection before it ever reached a test or CI). `update_reliable_ack()`'s own DATA-arrival first-contact fix from earlier in this milestone is untouched - still correct for the ordinary live-streaming case where DATA races ahead of the Heartbeat. **Tests**: `tests/test_heartbeat.c`'s pre-existing `test_heartbeat_first_contact_sets_baseline_with_no_data_ever_received()` (written before this Subscriber-requested-durability distinction existed) needed a conscious update - `sub.durable = true` added explicitly, since its own scenario (expects the full backlog range requested) is now specifically the durable case; a new sibling `test_heartbeat_first_contact_volatile_subscriber_skips_backlog()` covers the opposite, actually-default case, confirmed reproducing the exact leak (`ack_seq_no` stuck at `first_available_seq_no`, a real ACKNACK sent) before the fix, and closing it (synced to `last_seq_no + 1`, zero ACKNACKs, a later genuine gap still tracks normally) after. A real bug found writing this test's own `write_data()` helper (not a production bug): `tt_DataHeader.entity_id`'s own byte offset (16) overlaps `tt_HeartbeatHeader.flags`/`reserved[]` when both helpers reuse the same `node->rx_buffer` in one test - left unset, `entity_id` silently inherited stale nonzero bytes from an earlier `write_heartbeat()` call, splitting traffic across two different `struct tt_WriterProxy` entries instead of one and masking the test's own intended assertions; fixed by having `write_data()` set `entity_id = 0` explicitly rather than relying on incidental zero-initialization. Verified locally: `make test`/`make sanitize`/`make -C platform/freertos`/`clang-format --dry-run --Werror`/`clang-tidy` all clean; the new test independently confirmed to reproduce the exact leak when the fix is reverted, then pass again once restored. Real CI confirmed green (`28a9c39`: `Check all`/`Test all`/`Performance Test`/`rmw_tickle performance` all `success`).

**Final real-HIL re-verification, both remaining findings closed**: `durability_late_join` volatile - `received=0` exactly, 3/3 (was 57); durable - unaffected, 20/20 unchanged, 2/2. `history_depth_burst_loss` - `recv > sent` signal gone entirely, replaced by ordinary `recv < sent` (real loss only, e.g. 147/156/147 of 160 sent) - confirming (3)'s own root cause was this scenario's real driver all along, not (2)'s own bitmap-duplicate case as first guessed. One open, honestly-unresolved observation: `history_depth_burst_loss`'s own real loss count now varies run to run (13/4/13 lost, vs. CycloneDDS's own deterministic "52 lost" on the same scenario) - plausibly just real timing variance inherent to this scenario's own race (exactly when late-join matching completes relative to the Publisher's continuous send rate and the `depth=8` ring's own eviction), not yet root-caused further; left as-is rather than guessed at. `lifespan_expiry` - real loss only, no duplicate signal (from earlier verification, unaffected by this final fix). `COMPARISON.MD` updated by TickLE Plan with these final numbers (commit `51aaca1`). | 58, 59 | ✅ fully closed - all three real-HIL findings from Milestone 58's own to-do list resolved and confirmed on real hardware, real CI green throughout |

| **61** | ✅ **`tt_ReliableCache`'s own `depth` ceiling made caller-configurable - the "DDS semantic-parity backlog" row 2 task, at the user's own explicit decision, relayed via TickLE Plan** ("`resource_limits`처럼 늘릴 수 있게 변경... 임베디드 타겟이라 무한정은 아니겠지만... malloc 없는 설계 방식은 그대로 유지"). **Design, chosen entirely by TickLE Dev per the user's own delegation**: `struct tt_ReliableCache.entries[tt_MAX_RELIABLE_HISTORY]` (an embedded, fixed-size array - tickle.h) replaced with `entries` (a `struct tt_ReliableCacheEntry*`) + a new `capacity` field (the actual size of whatever backing array the caller provides) - the SAME caller-owned, no-malloc-inside-TickLE-core idiom `struct tt_Discovery` (`tt_Node_set_discovery()`) already established, not a new pattern. `depth` (1..`capacity`) stays a separate field from `capacity` on purpose, deliberately mirroring DDS's own separate `HISTORY.depth` vs `RESOURCE_LIMITS.max_samples` split - a caller can shrink `depth` at runtime (a plain field write) without touching the backing array. This makes `depth` a genuine *per-Publisher* ceiling (like real DDS `RESOURCE_LIMITS`) instead of a single build-wide constant every Publisher was capped by or paid for alike - an embedded-target Publisher can size its own array tiny, a high-throughput Linux one (or a real ROS 2 caller's own requested QoS depth, `rmw_tickle`) can size it far past the old `tt_MAX_RELIABLE_HISTORY`=64 default. `tickle.c`'s four call sites sharing the `(cache->depth > 0 && cache->depth <= tt_MAX_RELIABLE_HISTORY) ? cache->depth : tt_MAX_RELIABLE_HISTORY` clamp (`cache_reliable_sample()`, `reliable_cache_oldest_seq_no()`, `deliver_durability_backlog()`, `process_acknack()`) now clamp against `cache->capacity` instead, each gaining an explicit `capacity == 0` guard (a safe no-op, matching this file's own "not configured yet" conventions) for a cache whose caller forgot to set `entries`/`capacity` before use. `tt_MAX_RELIABLE_HISTORY` itself (`config.h`) is unchanged in value but narrows to two purely *Subscriber*-side roles now (documented in full in its own doc comment): `skip_unrecoverable_backlog()`'s own give-up heuristic (an educated guess about a *remote* Publisher's plausible depth, not an enforced bound anymore) and examples/tests' own default array size when they have no other reason to pick something different - unaffected by a specific Publisher's own now-independent `capacity`.
**Honest, load-bearing limitation found while implementing this and stated plainly rather than implying "just raise depth" fixes everything (the user was told this and asked to proceed with full knowledge of it)**: raising a Publisher's own depth past `tt_RELIABLE_BITMAP_BITS` (64) only ever helps DURABILITY's own one-shot backlog push (`deliver_durability_backlog()` walks `entries[]` directly, no ACKNACK/bitmap involved) - it does **not**, by itself, improve RELIABLE's own ACKNACK-driven recovery under sustained loss at high throughput, because `struct tt_WriterProxy.received_bitmap` (the *Subscriber's* own out-of-order tracking) is a fixed `uint64_t`: once more than 64 newer samples arrive while one gap stays unresolved, `update_reliable_ack()` is forced to `jump_ack_baseline()` and abandon that gap outright, regardless of how deep the Publisher's own cache still reaches back. At TickLE's own real measured throughput (~1.2M msg/s), 64 samples pass in roughly 53 microseconds - almost certainly shorter than one real ACKNACK round trip on any real network - so this Subscriber-side ceiling, not Publisher-side cache depth, is very likely the actual bottleneck behind TickLE's own comparatively poor tc-loss recovery at high throughput (`rmw_tickle/COMPARISON.MD`). Closing that would need a genuinely different Subscriber-side tracking mechanism (e.g. more than one simultaneously-open gap window) - out of this milestone's own scope, documented directly in `struct tt_ReliableCache`'s own doc comment and `tt_MAX_RELIABLE_HISTORY`'s own doc comment (both `tickle.h`/`config.h`) so a future reader hits the same honest answer, not a repeat investigation.
**Every construction site updated** (the struct no longer embeds its own array, so every caller now declares one): `examples/perf_hil/tickle/{reliable_latency/client.c,reliable_latency/server.c,history_depth_burst_loss/client.c}` (unchanged depth 8, own `static` backing array - zero-initialized for free, C's own static-storage-duration guarantee); `{durability_late_join/server.c,lifespan_expiry/client.c}` (unchanged depth `tt_MAX_RELIABLE_HISTORY`, same); `examples/linux/perf/perf_client.c` (the actual `run_perf.sh` HIL tool) and `examples/perf_hil/tickle/reliable_throughput/client.c` (the scenario TickLE Plan will re-measure) both gained a new, generously-sized `MAX_RELIABLE_DEPTH=8192` backing array and let their own pre-existing `-K <depth>` flag (already present on the former; added to the latter, mirroring it) reach genuinely past the old 64 ceiling instead of only up to it - `reliable_throughput/client.c`'s own `RESULT:` line also now reports `reliable_depth=` for direct correlation with whatever `-K` value a given re-measurement run used. `rmw_tickle/rmw_tickle/src/rmw_publisher.c` - the one caller that already accepts dynamic allocation (unlike every TickLE-core-facing example above) - now allocates `entries[]` via a second `allocator->zero_allocate()` sized to *whatever* real depth the ROS 2 caller requested (`qos_profile->depth`), removing the old hard rejection past `tt_MAX_RELIABLE_HISTORY` entirely (replaced by a rejection only at the genuine `uint16_t` type-width ceiling, `UINT16_MAX`) - matching real `rmw_fastrtps_cpp`/`rmw_cyclonedds_cpp`'s own dynamic-depth support instead of trailing it; `rmw_destroy_publisher()` frees `entries` before the struct that pointed at it. The three new sequential allocate-or-fail branches this added pushed `rmw_create_publisher()`'s own cognitive complexity over clang-tidy's threshold - split the whole depth-computation/allocation block into its own `setup_reliable_cache()` static helper (returning `bool`, the caller doing one shared cleanup-and-return-NULL instead of the same block repeated three times), fixing the warning and also removing the triplicated cleanup code in the same pass. `tests/{test_durability_pubsub.c,test_heartbeat.c,test_reliable_pubsub.c}` - all 22 `struct tt_ReliableCache` construction sites (a scripted transformation, not manual, given the count) gained their own `struct tt_ReliableCacheEntry <name>_entries[N]` backing array; a real bug found doing this the first pass, not a production one: a *local* (stack) `entries[]` array isn't zero-initialized the way the old embedded-in-a-`memset()`'d-struct array implicitly was, so 5 tests failed with inflated counts (stale stack garbage in `entries[i].len` read as "occupied") until an explicit `memset(<name>_entries, 0, sizeof(<name>_entries))` was added at every site - caught immediately by the very next `make test` run, not shipped.
**Verified**: `make test`/`make sanitize` (ASan+UBSan, would have caught the stack-zero-init bug above even without the explicit test-count check)/`make -C platform/freertos`/`clang-format --dry-run --Werror`/`clang-tidy` (bare invocation) all clean on every touched TickLE-core file; `rmw_tickle` rebuilt clean (`colcon build --packages-select rmw_tickle`) and its own full test suite passes (20/20 - an initial run showed 4 crashes, confirmed via `git stash` to be pre-existing/environment-flaky and identical with or without this milestone's own changes, resolved by a clean rebuild, not a regression this milestone introduced); `clang-tidy -p build/rmw_tickle` on `rmw_publisher.c` clean (0 warnings, including confirming the cognitive-complexity split actually fixed the threshold violation) after real compile-database verification, not the flag-less fallback. Real CI confirmed green (`6a5c061`: `Check all`/`Test all`/`Performance Test`/`rmw_tickle performance` all `success`).

**Real HIL re-measurement (TickLE Plan) confirmed the honest finding above exactly**: `-K 512`/`-K 8192` at 1%/5% tc loss both landed in the same range as the old `depth=64` baseline (2.6-5.4% vs 1.1-5.3%) - no RELIABLE recovery improvement from a deeper cache, and server logs showed `"Reliable gap too large to track (64 ahead of N)"` regardless of `-K`'s own value, direct confirmation the Subscriber's own 64-bit `received_bitmap` ceiling (not Publisher cache depth) is what's actually gating recovery. **A second, unpredicted finding**: `-K 8192` at 5% loss measured *worse* than `-K 512` (7.5-7.6% vs 5.1-5.4%, 2/2 reproduced) - not predicted by the honest-limitation analysis above, which only argued "no improvement," not "actively worse." **Root-caused after the fact, from the code itself, not left a mystery**: `find_resendable_cache_entry()` (`process_acknack()`'s own per-ACKNACK-bit lookup, called up to `tt_RELIABLE_BITMAP_BITS`=64 times per ACKNACK) does a plain **O(depth) linear scan** over `cache->entries[0..depth)` checking `seq_no` equality - unlike the write side (`cache_reliable_sample()`), which computes its own ring slot directly (`cache->next % depth`, O(1)), the read side never mirrors that: it never computes where a given `seq_no` *should* be, it walks the whole array checking every slot.
