At `depth=64` this is cheap and fits comfortably in any real cache; at `depth=8192`, `struct tt_ReliableCacheEntry`'s own `tt_MAX_BUFFER_LENGTH`-sized (1472-byte) `buffer` member means the full `entries[]` array is ~12MB - far past any realistic L2/L3 - so a single ACKNACK with many set bits can trigger up to 64 separate ~8192-entry scans through that 12MB region, almost entirely cache misses. This is a real, structural inefficiency confirmed directly by reading the code (not a guess), and a well-grounded, though not separately profiled, explanation for the measured degradation - `find_resendable_cache_entry()` could compute a direct index the same way the write side already does (`missing_seq_no`'s own expected ring slot, verified by checking that slot's own `seq_no` matches rather than scanning for it), an O(1) fix that would need no depth-related trade-off at all. Not implemented here - out of this milestone's own original scope, flagged as a real, concrete, ready-to-scope follow-up rather than left unexplained. `COMPARISON.MD` §6 item 9 / this row updated by TickLE Plan with the real numbers (commit `2128839`). | 2 (DDS semantic-parity backlog) | ✅ implemented, real CI green, and real-HIL re-measurement confirmed the honest finding exactly - plus found and root-caused a genuine, separate `O(depth)` lookup-cost regression at large depth, not yet fixed (a real, scoped follow-up candidate: convert `find_resendable_cache_entry()` to a direct-index lookup) |

| **62** | ✅ **Three DDS semantic-parity backlog items, all at the user's own direct confirmation ("진행해줘") after TickLE Plan's own relay explicitly deferred to it - a peer's "사용자님이 승인했다" claim was not itself treated as authorization, matching this session's own established discipline.** **(1) Row 1 - `process_acknack()` gated on `pub->reliable`.** The user's own decision, corrected mid-relay (first "keep as-is", then explicitly "gate by `pub->reliable`, not `pub->durable`"): a BEST_EFFORT (non-reliable) Publisher should never answer an ACKNACK's own *retransmission* request from `reliable_cache`, even though Milestone 24's unified cache means a DURABLE-but-not-RELIABLE Publisher still has one allocated (durability's own backlog is already served entirely by `deliver_durability_backlog()`'s one-shot push, no ACKNACK involved). Gating on `pub->durable` instead was explicitly rejected (this row's own earlier analysis) since it would break the legitimate in-flight-loss recovery a VOLATILE+RELIABLE Publisher must still guarantee an already-matched Subscriber; `pub->reliable` alone has no such risk - a Publisher that never offered RELIABLE was never obligated to answer that request in the first place, and `subscriber_incompatible_with_publisher()`'s own RxO gate already refuses to match a reliable-requesting Subscriber against one anyway. **Real design subtlety found and corrected while implementing, not part of the original ask**: the new gate must wrap *only* the retransmission loop, not the whole function - `peer_ack_seq_no[]`'s own update (the aggregation `tt_Publisher_wait_for_all_acked()` is built on) stays unconditional on `reliable_cache != NULL` alone, matching that function's own existing doc-commented contract ("best-effort has no ack state to solicit... reliable_cache still NULL"), since a DURABLE-only Publisher can legitimately solicit and track an ACKNACK reply too (`tt_Publisher_request_ack()`'s own Heartbeat, answered regardless of `pub->reliable`) - an initial, wider gate placement broke this and was caught immediately by `test_process_acknack_updates_peer_ack_seq_no()`. The new `pub->reliable` check itself tipped `process_acknack()`'s own cognitive complexity over clang-tidy's threshold - the retransmit loop's own body was split into a new `retransmit_reliable_samples()` static helper, same "split out purely to keep complexity down" pattern this file already uses repeatedly. **Two real, pre-existing test gaps found and fixed, not new bugs this milestone introduced**: `tests/test_reliable_pubsub.c`'s own `test_process_acknack_retransmits_cached_sample()` (the *primary* "does retransmission work at all" test) and `test_process_acknack_skips_expired_sample()`, plus `tests/test_durability_pubsub.c`'s own `test_durability_backlog_recovered_via_acknack_when_reliable_too()` (its own name says "when reliable too") all constructed a Publisher with `reliable_cache` set but never actually set `pub.reliable = true` - passing "by accident" purely off the old, weaker `reliable_cache != NULL` check for as long as it existed; the new, correct gate immediately exposed all three (one as a real test failure, two as "passing for the wrong reason" until manually re-checked), fixed by adding the missing line each already should have had. New `test_process_acknack_ignored_for_durable_only_publisher()` pins the actual behavior change directly (a durable-but-not-reliable Publisher with a real, populated cache must not answer). **(2) Row 2's own real-HIL-found follow-up - `find_resendable_cache_entry()` converted from an O(depth) linear scan to O(1) direct indexing.** `cache_reliable_sample()`'s own write side always places seq_no N at `entries[(N - 1) % depth]` (`cache->next` starts at 0, a Publisher's first seq_no is always 1, both advance one slot per publish in lockstep) - the read side is rewritten to compute that same slot directly instead of scanning `entries[0..depth)` for a match, closing the exact `O(depth)`-per-ACKNACK-bit cost Milestone 61's own real-HIL re-measurement traced the `depth=8192`-is-worse-than-`depth=512` regression to. Depends on `depth` staying the same between the write that placed a sample and this lookup for it - true for every current caller (never changed mid-stream by anything in this codebase today) but not structurally enforced; the write side's own `cache->next % depth` indexing already silently relies on the identical assumption, so this isn't a new risk. New `test_process_acknack_direct_index_correct_after_wraparound()` pins the exact ring-wraparound shape that would expose a wrong-slot bug (depth=4, 6 samples published so the ring has wrapped once) across three distinct cases: an evicted seq_no whose own slot now holds a *different*, newer one (must not false-match it), a wrapped-into slot, and an un-wrapped one - each verified via exactly one `entries[].retry` counter incrementing, not merely that some retransmit happened. Confirmed both ways: reverting to a deliberately-wrong index formula (`missing_seq_no % depth` instead of `(missing_seq_no - 1) % depth`) makes this new test (and the pre-existing basic retransmit test) fail immediately, restoring the correct formula makes the whole suite pass again. **(3) Row 3 - LIVELINESS made genuinely per-entity-lease-aware.** New public `tt_Node_entity_alive(node, entity, now)` (`tickle.h`/`tickle.c`) computes an entity's own liveliness fresh at query time instead of only ever reflecting `check_liveliness()`'s own coarse ~3s node-level sweep: if the entity never requested a specific `liveliness_lease_duration_ns` (the common/default case), it returns `.alive` verbatim, deferring entirely to that existing sweep (a genuine participant-level "did this whole node disappear" signal, correctly left unchanged); if it *did* request one, the answer is computed directly from `node->update_last_seen[entity->node_id]` against that entity's own lease, bypassing `.alive`/the sweep's own timing entirely - authoritative for a leased entity even while the slower sweep hasn't caught up yet. `rmw_tickle/rmw_tickle/src/rmw_graph.c`'s own `count_matching_locked()`/`count_not_alive_matching_locked()` (feeding `check_subscription_liveliness()`'s own *already*-correct per-lease polling cadence, `rmw_subscription.c` - this row's own earlier investigation confirmed that cadence was already right, core just had nothing faster to poll *for*) now call the new function instead of reading `.alive` directly - the actual end-to-end fix, since exposing the new core capability alone would have been inert without a real caller consuming it. Four *other* `.alive` checks in the same file (general topic/node graph introspection - `rmw_get_publishers_info_by_topic()` and similar) were deliberately left untouched, a different, eventually-consistent-snapshot concern than this row's own specific RMW_EVENT_LIVELINESS_CHANGED focus - not fixed here to keep the change appropriately scoped, not an oversight. **Honest scope limit, stated plainly**: this sharpens AUTOMATIC liveliness's own existing timing only (same underlying "any UPDATE from this node" signal, now checked per-entity-lease instead of one shared window) - it does not separately implement MANUAL_BY_TOPIC's own stricter real-DDS semantic (an entity must itself specifically publish/assert within its lease, not merely share a node with other traffic), which would need a genuinely new, per-entity activity signal TickLE's wire protocol doesn't carry today. Five new whitebox tests in `tests/test_liveliness.c` cover the zero-lease-defers-to-.alive case (both directions), the fresh-computation boundary (exactly at the lease, one ns past it), the leased-entity-ignores-a-stale-true-.alive-flag case (the actual point of this fix), a never-seen-node-id, and an empty/invalid discovery slot - confirmed to fail against a deliberately reverted "just return `.alive` always" implementation, pass against the real one. **Verified**: `make test`/`make sanitize` (ASan+UBSan)/`make -C platform/freertos`/`clang-format --dry-run --Werror`/`clang-tidy` (bare invocation) all clean on every touched TickLE-core file; `rmw_tickle` rebuilt clean (`colcon build --packages-select rmw_tickle`, `-DCMAKE_EXPORT_COMPILE_COMMANDS=ON` for a real `clang-tidy -p build/rmw_tickle` pass, 0 warnings) - its own test suite showed the same 4-test crash cluster (`test_events`/`test_graph`/`test_liveliness_lost_watchdog`/`test_publish_take_reuse`) seen during Milestone 61, re-confirmed via the identical `git stash`-and-rebuild method to be genuine pre-existing sandbox flakiness (identical failure with or without every one of this milestone's own changes, including with them entirely stashed back to Milestone 61's own baseline) - real, multi-second, real-threading/mutex tests inherently more exposed to this sandbox's own timing quirks than the rest of the suite, not a regression. Real CI confirmed green (`b42f08f`: `Check all`/`Test all`/`Performance Test`/`rmw_tickle performance` all `success`) - `Check all`'s own real rmw_tickle test environment showed 20/20 passing (0 errors, 0 failures), independently corroborating the sandbox-flakiness conclusion above rather than just trusting the local `git stash` comparison alone. | 1, 2, 3 (DDS semantic-parity backlog) | ✅ implemented, real CI green |

| **63** | ✅ **Milestone 62 row 3's own native-observability follow-up, at the user's own direct confirmation ("2번으로 진행하자") after TickLE Plan flagged a real gap before re-measuring `COMPARISON.MD` scenario 8.** TickLE Plan noticed, ahead of re-measuring, that `examples/perf_hil/tickle/liveliness_loss_detection/server.c`'s own TickLE-native scenario reads `is_departed` from `discovery_callback`, and that callback's `departed=true` was fired *only* by `check_liveliness()`'s existing fixed ~3s node-level sweep (`tombstone_discovered_entities_from_source()`) - Milestone 62's new `tt_Node_entity_alive()` was never actually called from that path, only from `rmw_tickle`'s own `count_matching_locked()`/`count_not_alive_matching_locked()`. Independently confirmed by grep/read before acting (not taken on the peer's word alone, per this session's own standing discipline) - `tt_Node_entity_alive` genuinely had zero call sites in `check_liveliness()`'s own callers. TickLE Plan offered two options (leave native as node-level-only-by-design vs. extend the callback path too) and the user picked the latter. **Implementation**: a new `tombstone_entities_past_own_lease(node, time)` (`src/tickle.c`, placed immediately before `check_liveliness()`) iterates `node->discovery->entities[]` directly (not by node id, since distinct entities sharing one node id may have different leases) and, for each `alive` entity with a non-zero `liveliness_lease_duration_ns`, calls `tt_Node_entity_alive()` against the same `time` `check_liveliness()` already has - tombstoning (`alive = false`, `discovery_callback(..., departed=true, ...)`) any that have gone stale relative to their own lease, independent of whether the whole owning node is presumed dead. Called once per `check_liveliness()` tick (still `tt_NODE_UPDATE_INTERVAL`, ~1s) right after the existing node-level loop - not a new poll loop, reusing the existing per-second cadence, so a leased entity's own departure is now detected within about one tick of its own lease boundary instead of only ever at the fixed ~3s node-wide mark (bounded by this ~1s granularity, not sub-second - `rmw_tickle`'s own `tt_Node_entity_alive()`-based polling, which reschedules at the lease period itself, stays the finer-grained consumer for that layer). Entities with no lease (`liveliness_lease_duration_ns == 0`) are untouched - `tt_Node_entity_alive()` itself defers those to `.alive`, unaffected by this change, matching Milestone 62's own zero-lease behavior exactly. Order-safe against the existing node-level loop in the same tick: an entity already tombstoned by that loop (its owning node presumed fully dead) is skipped here (`!entity->alive` guard) rather than double-firing the callback. **Tests**: two new whitebox cases in `tests/test_discovery.c` (plus a small `write_update_one_entity_with_lease()` helper, same shape as `test_rxo_matching.c`'s own `write_update_one_subscriber_full()` but kept file-local) - `test_short_lease_entity_tombstoned_before_node_level_sweep()` (a 500ns-lease entity fires `departed=true` at `check_liveliness(node, 501, NULL)` while the owning node's own `update_seen[]` stays `true`, proving the node-level path didn't do it) and its contrast pair `test_short_lease_entity_not_tombstoned_before_its_own_lease()` (exactly at the lease boundary, no callback fires). **Verified**: `make test`/`make sanitize` (ASan+UBSan)/`make -C platform/freertos`/`clang-format --dry-run --Werror`/`clang-tidy` (bare invocation, `src/tickle.c` and `tests/test_discovery.c` both 0 warnings) all clean; `rmw_tickle` rebuilt clean (`rm -rf build/rmw_tickle install/rmw_tickle` then `colcon build --packages-select rmw_tickle`) with its own full test suite passing 20/20 (no crash cluster this run - the ROS environment needed `source /opt/ros/*/setup.bash` in the same shell invocation as `colcon test`, a tool-session shell-persistence gotcha, not a real flakiness recurrence) - confirms `rmw_node.c`'s own `discovery_callback()` consumer (`(void)departed;`, only using the callback to signal a generic "something changed" guard condition) is unaffected by the extra tombstone firings this milestone adds. Real CI confirmed green (`d05cd07`: `Check all`/`Test all`/`rmw_tickle performance` all `success` on the first push; `Performance Test` failed twice with total 0-msgs-received across every scenario including ones that never touch this milestone's own code path - root-caused, not guessed, via direct SSH to both rig Pis using `run_perf.sh`'s own CI key: `eth0` showed `NO-CARRIER`/`state DOWN` on both, the dedicated point-to-point test-link the perf traffic actually uses, a separate interface from the `wlan0`-based management network the earlier ping/ssh checks were unknowingly using - a genuine rig hardware flap, independently reproduced by TickLE Plan's own check, self-recovered without physical intervention, then `Performance Test` reran green). | 62 (row 3), 3 (DDS semantic-parity backlog) | ✅ implemented, real CI green |

| **64** | ✅ **Both real gaps from the "DDS QoS policy coverage inventory" section, in the user's own explicit work order (item 1 first, then item 2), at direct confirmation each time - a peer relay never counted as authorization, same standing discipline this whole session used throughout.** **(1) `HISTORY.KEEP_ALL` for Publishers, no longer silently downgraded.** `rmw_tickle_validate_qos_profile()` (`rmw_qos.c`) only ever rejected KEEP_ALL for Subscriptions - a Publisher requesting it passed validation, then `setup_reliable_cache()` (`rmw_publisher.c`) never read `qos_profile->history` at all, only `->depth` (falling back to `tt_MAX_RELIABLE_HISTORY`=64 if unset), silently downgrading a "retain everything" request to an ordinary small bounded cache - a real violation of this file's own stated philosophy ("rejects anything outside the currently-supported set explicitly... rather than silently downgrading it"). At the user's own explicit choice: honored instead of rejected - a new `RMW_TICKLE_KEEP_ALL_DEPTH`=8192 default (matching `MAX_RELIABLE_DEPTH` in `examples/perf_hil/tickle/reliable_throughput/client.c`'s own established convention, ~12.2MB per KEEP_ALL Publisher, the same already-measured `-K 8192` figure `COMPARISON.MD` §6 item 9/10 already found - not a new cost) used instead of `->depth` whenever `qos_profile->history == KEEP_ALL`. Subscription-side KEEP_ALL rejection in `rmw_qos.c` stays exactly as-is, unaffected. New `tests/test_history_keep_all.c` exercises the real `rmw_create_publisher()` path (not a whitebox include) for both RELIABLE and TRANSIENT_LOCAL Publishers requesting KEEP_ALL, plus a KEEP_LAST contrast case proving ordinary `->depth` sizing is unaffected. **(2) `BEST_AVAILABLE`, the only gap needing new behavior, not just a sizing choice.** New `rmw_tickle_resolve_best_available()` (`rmw_qos.c`) resolves every `RMW_QOS_POLICY_*_BEST_AVAILABLE` value (reliability/durability/liveliness) and the two `_BEST_AVAILABLE` duration sentinels (deadline/liveliness_lease_duration, `RMW_DURATION_INFINITE - 1`, previously silently mis-handled as a literal ~292-year value) once, at entity-creation time, against `context_impl->discovery`'s own currently-known entities on the same topic - matching every `_BEST_AVAILABLE` enumerator/sentinel's own doc comment in `rmw/types.h` verbatim (resolved once, never re-evaluated later, even if incompatible endpoints appear afterward). **Real design insight found while implementing, derived directly from this same file's own existing `rmw_qos_profile_check_compatible()` rules, not guessed**: for RELIABILITY/DURABILITY/LIVELINESS, an *offering* Publisher always resolves trivially to the strict value (RELIABLE/TRANSIENT_LOCAL/MANUAL_BY_TOPIC) without needing to scan discovery at all, since offering strict is RxO-compatible with *any* Subscription request (`rmw_qos_profile_check_compatible()`'s own incompatibility rules only ever fire on a *loose*-offering Publisher against a *strict*-requesting Subscription); only a *requesting* Subscription's own resolution genuinely depends on discovery, and only then does it need to fall back to loose (when even one discovered Publisher doesn't offer strict). For DEADLINE/LIVELINESS_LEASE_DURATION, `rmw/types.h`'s own doc comment is explicitly asymmetric instead (both sides compute a real, discovery-dependent value): a Subscription takes the *maximum* of every discovered Publisher's own value (the loosest bound still compatible with all of them); a Publisher takes the *minimum* of every discovered Subscription's own value (only as strict as the tightest real requirement demands). A discovered entity's own `0` ("no requirement", `struct tt_DiscoveredEntity`'s own existing documented convention) is substituted with "unconstrained" for this computation - it never wins a min (an unconstrained peer shouldn't narrow anything), but always wins a max (staying compatible with an unconstrained peer requires matching its own lack of a bound too) - converted back to `0` on the way out if the final result is still unconstrained (every matching entity was itself unconstrained, or none matched at all). Wired into `rmw_create_publisher()`/`rmw_create_subscription()` by reassigning the `qos_profile` parameter itself to a resolved local copy right after `node_impl`/`context_impl` become available (under `node_mutex`, the same lock every other `discovery.entities[]` reader in this package already requires) - every downstream reference in either function needed no further edits. New `tests/test_best_available.c` (24 assertions) covers the enum-resolution algorithm (including the "Publisher always trivially strict, proven with zero discovered peers" case and the Subscription vacuous-truth/fallback cases) and the DEADLINE/LIVELINESS_LEASE_DURATION min/max asymmetry including both "0 = unconstrained" edge cases (collapses a max, gets skipped by a min) - confirmed by the real test run, not just reasoned about (the carefully-derived asymmetric design passed on the very first clean build). **Verified**: `rmw_tickle` rebuilt clean (`rm -rf build/rmw_tickle install/rmw_tickle` then `colcon build --packages-select rmw_tickle`) for both changes independently, full test suite passing 24/24 (0 errors/failures) after each, `clang-tidy -p build/rmw_tickle` 0 warnings on every touched file (two real `misc-include-cleaner`/`readability-identifier-length` warnings and a batch of `readability-magic-numbers`/`readability-math-missing-parentheses` warnings in the new BEST_AVAILABLE test were found and fixed, not suppressed - a direct `<tickle/tickle.h>` include, longer variable names, named duration constants instead of inline arithmetic literals, and explicit parens around mixed `+`/`*` comparisons), `clang-format --dry-run --Werror` clean. Real CI confirmed green (`abcffa1`: `Check all` `success` directly; `Test all`/`Performance Test`/`rmw_tickle performance` all `success` on `d7addbf`, a docs-only fast-forward TickLE Plan pushed immediately on top with zero code diff, so its own green run covers `abcffa1`'s code unchanged - `Performance Test`/`rmw_tickle performance`'s own `concurrency: group: perf-main, cancel-in-progress: true` setting cancelled `abcffa1`'s own in-flight runs for those two workflows specifically when the newer push landed, `Test all` has no such grouping and ran both to completion independently). | DDS QoS policy coverage inventory, gaps 1/2 | ✅ implemented, real CI green |

| **65** | ✅ **ACKNACK bitmap widened 64 -> 256 bits, the "TickLE-native performance" plan's own highest-leverage fix for scenario 4's measured RELIABLE tc-loss recovery gap, at the user's own direct confirmation relayed via TickLE Plan ("사용자님이 ACKNACK 비트맵을 256비트로 확장하기로 최종 결정하셨습니다").** Already-diagnosed root cause (this same plan's own earlier analysis, `struct tt_ReliableCache`'s own doc comment): `struct tt_WriterProxy.received_bitmap` was a bare `uint64_t` (`tt_RELIABLE_BITMAP_BITS`=64) - at TickLE's own real throughput (~1.2M msg/s), 64 samples pass in ~53us, almost certainly shorter than one real ACKNACK round trip, forcing `jump_ack_baseline()` to abandon any gap wider than that regardless of Publisher-side cache depth (already ruled out as the bottleneck by Milestone 61's own real-HIL re-measurement). **Implementation**: `struct tt_AckNackHeader.bitmap`/`struct tt_WriterProxy.received_bitmap` both widened from `uint64_t` to a `tt_RELIABLE_BITMAP_WORDS`-word (4) `uint64_t` array (256 bits total, matching real RTPS's own practical SequenceNumberSet width) - a real wire-protocol layout change, `tt_VERSION` bumped 4 -> 5 (no partial-compatibility case to handle, matching every prior bump's own precedent - `process_packet()` already rejects any older-version peer outright). A new, small, fixed set of `bitmap_*()` helpers (`src/tickle.c`, next to the renamed `highest_received_bit()` -> `bitmap_highest_bit()`) replace every single-word `uint64_t` bit expression the file used before the widening - `bitmap_is_zero`/`_clear`/`_lowest_bit_set`/`_test_bit`/`_set_bit`/`_shift_right_one`/`_shift_right`/`_highest_bit`/`_low_mask` - each `O(word-count)`, not `O(bit-count)`, except `bitmap_shift_right()` (a genuine cross-word carry at an arbitrary bit offset needs it, but the shift amount stays bounded by the fixed, small `tt_RELIABLE_BITMAP_BITS` width, not by anything that scales with cache depth or config the way Milestone 61's own `O(depth)` regression did - the exact caution TickLE Plan's own design note called out in advance). `retransmit_reliable_samples()`'s own per-bit retransmit loop now skips whole zero words before touching `find_resendable_cache_entry()` at all, the same "stay `O(word-count)`" discipline applied to the one call site that caution specifically flagged. **Real bug found and fixed while updating tests, not shipped**: `tests/test_reliable_pubsub.c`'s own `test_acknack_retry_bulk_skip_matches_depth_vs_bitmap_width()` had a dormant branch (`bulk_skip_possible`) that had never actually run before, since `tt_MAX_RELIABLE_HISTORY + 6 < tt_RELIABLE_BITMAP_BITS` was always false at the old 64-bit width - widening the bitmap flipped it live for the first time, immediately exposing that the test's own bit-seeding expression (`1ULL << (far_seq - 1)`) is undefined behavior once `far_seq - 1` can exceed 63 (which this same widening makes reachable) - fixed by using `bitmap_set_bit()`/`bitmap_test_bit()` (arbitrary bit position, no raw shift) instead, in both this test and its own still-dead-with-today's-constants `else` branch (latent the same way, fixed defensively). **Verified**: `make test`/`make sanitize` (ASan+UBSan)/`make -C platform/freertos`/`clang-format --dry-run --Werror`/`clang-tidy` (bare invocation, 0 warnings after renaming every short loop variable clang-tidy's own `readability-identifier-length`/`readability-magic-numbers` checks flagged - `w`/`b`/`n` aren't in this file's own `.clang-tidy` short-name allowlist, and bare `64`/`63` literals needed a new named `tt_RELIABLE_BITMAP_WORD_BITS` constant, config.h) all clean; `rmw_tickle` rebuilt clean (24/24 tests) - unaffected, since it never touches these wire structs directly; every `examples/perf_hil/tickle/*/client.c,server.c` scenario compiles clean against the widened headers. **Real HIL confirmed the fix, not just the theory - re-measured once to rule out a separate confound, not taken on the first run alone**: an initial measurement (commit `cdac082`) already showed RELIABLE `loss_pct` at 0.0% for both 1%/5% injected tc loss, but TickLE Plan flagged, before it was written up here as final, that this same `Performance Test` run predated their own separate `reliable_throughput/server.c` harness fix (`474e755` - a real `recv > sent` duplicate-counting bug, unrelated to this milestone's own core change, but one that could plausibly inflate `recv` and mask real loss in the same measurement). Re-measured on commit `1c3b232` (this milestone's own PLAN.md write-up commit, which already sits on top of that harness fix) instead of trusting the earlier number: RELIABLE `loss_pct` at 1%/5% injected tc loss is still **0.0%** (full recovery, confirmed clean of the harness confound) - down from the previously-measured 1.1-5.3% residual (`COMPARISON.MD` §3/§6 item 7), exceeding even the "likely closes most or all of it" honest expectation stated going in. `run_perf.sh`'s own loss matrix also covers 10% (not previously called out in this plan's own analysis): a small residual, 0.3%, remains there - the real link's own ACKNACK RTT, not the bitmap window, is expected to be the limit at some loss rate, and this may be it, though not separately diagnosed. All 4 real CI workflows (`Check all`/`Test all`/`Performance Test`/`rmw_tickle performance`) confirmed green for `1c3b232`. **Correction (2026-09-22) - the 0.0% figure itself, even harness-confound-free, was still the wrong measurement to cite here, found by TickLE Plan and independently verified**: it came from `run_perf.sh`'s own `perf_client`/`perf_server -R` tool, "a modest (~500 message) sample at `LOSS_TEST_INTERVAL_SEC`'s own pacing" (that workflow's own comment) - a real, correctly-measured number, but for a different, much lower-rate tool than this section's own scenario 4 (`examples/perf_hil/tickle/reliable_throughput`, TickLE's real ~150-200K msg/s max send rate, no pacing). Re-measured with the actual scenario 4 methodology once the bitmap fix landed: real, reproducible residual loss remains at TickLE's own true max throughput - **3.0% at 1% injected tc loss, 6.95% at 5%** (`COMPARISON.MD` §3 scenario 4's own updated TickLE column; a 2-rep spread of 1.9-4.1%/5.8-8.1% was also observed, §6 item 11) - a real improvement over the old 64-bit baseline (was 2.1%/5.25%, later measured 1.1-3.1%/5.2-5.3% across reps) but **not** the full recovery this milestone's own "still 0.0%" claim above stated. Both the 0.0% and the 3.0%/6.95% numbers are real and correctly measured for what each actually tests; the honest summary is that the 256-bit window fully recovers loss at a modest, paced rate, but not yet at TickLE's own real max throughput - see `COMPARISON.MD` §6 item 11 for the full writeup, including a further poll-loop I/O-interleave experiment (not merged) that reproducibly improves real throughput without fully closing this residual either. | TickLE-native performance plan (widen the ACKNACK tracking window) | ✅ implemented, real CI green; real HIL loss_pct is 0.0% at the CI benchmark's own low pacing but 3.0%/6.95% (1%/5% tc loss) at TickLE's real max throughput - not a full recovery there, see 2026-09-22 correction above and `COMPARISON.MD` §6 item 11 |
| **66** | ✅ **Rebuilt the public perf dashboard (`https://tsnlab.github.io/tickle/dev/bench/`) around `COMPARISON.MD` §2-3's own TickLE-core HIL methodology, at the user's own direct three-point request ("benchmark 페이지를 일괄 수정하려해").** The dashboard's "Raspberry Pi (HIL, arm64)" section (both `dashboard.py`'s custom status grid and every `github-action-benchmark` graph) had tracked `run_perf.sh`'s own older tool (`examples/linux/perf/{perf_client,perf_server}`, `examples/linux/ping_pong`) - a different, lower-pacing methodology than `COMPARISON.MD`'s own real numbers, and directly implicated in Milestone 65's own overclaim above (a wrong-tool measurement, not a wrong-methodology bug in the tool itself, but the two documents silently tracking different things is exactly what let that happen unnoticed). **Scope, confirmed with the user via two rounds of `AskUserQuestion`**: TickLE-core's own 9 `examples/perf_hil/tickle/*` scenarios only (label stays "arm64"; `rmw_tickle`'s own cross-host numbers stay out - would need real ROS 2 provisioning on the HIL Pis, blocked on no passwordless `sudo` beyond the existing `tc` exception); all 10 old dashboard categories replaced outright, not kept alongside (a different tool's history in the same graph would be misleading); both the status-grid's row history and every graph's own history capped at 100 (`dashboard.py`'s `HISTORY_LIMIT`, `github-action-benchmark`'s own `max-items-in-chart` input - confirmed via that action's own README that this actively prunes stored history, not just the chart's own display). **Implementation**: `run_perf.sh` rewritten to build and run all 9 scenarios on both Pis (15 invocations total: 5 single-run scenarios, `durability_late_join` x2 (-D/default, since each RESULT line only reports one run's own `received` count - no combined durable/volatile line exists, confirmed directly against the source, not assumed), `history_depth_burst_loss` x2 (within/beyond the depth=8/interval=0.05s=0.4s eviction window - "within" uses `-p 0`, the simplest defensible choice since no prior automated run of this scenario ever existed to recover an exact value from), `reliable_throughput` x3 tc-loss levels (0/1/5%, not the old tool's 10% - matching `COMPARISON.MD` §3's own rows exactly), `liveliness_loss_detection` x3 lease values, `lifespan_expiry` x3 pause values matching `COMPARISON.MD` §6 item 8's own already-verified slope methodology), parsing each scenario's own uniform `RESULT: field=value ...` line with one generic extractor instead of nine bespoke regex blocks. `performance.yml`'s 7 old `benchmark-action` steps replaced with 11 new ones (one per scenario/direction), each capped at `max-items-in-chart: 100`; only the two throughput groups gate the build (`fail-on-alert`), matching the old workflow's own gating scope. `rmw-perf.yml` lost only its 2 `benchmark-action` steps (dashboard graphs), build/test/step-summary/artifact upload untouched. `dashboard.py`'s `HISTORY_LIMIT` 30 -> 100, the status-grid's Raspberry Pi column set redesigned around the new 11-field fragment (latency/throughput/loss/liveliness/lifespan/history-depth/durability), replacing the old throughput/RTT/small-msg/loss-pair set. **Three real bugs found only once run against the actual rig, not caught by local verification (`bash -n`, synthetic `RESULT:`-line JSON generation, `python3 -m py_compile`, a `merge`/`inject` dry run against synthetic `status.json` - all passed before the first push, none of them able to catch these classes of bug)**: (1) the very first live run's `Performance Test` job "succeeded" but every one of the 15 scenario invocations produced an all-N/A result, each completing in ~0.5s instead of several seconds - root cause: `run_scenario()` SSHed into rpi#1 and asked *it* to run `run_scenario.sh`, but that script is itself the orchestrator and already SSHes to both Pis on its own using whatever host invokes it (meant to run directly on the CI runner, where the real `~/.ssh/tickle_ci_ed25519` key lives) - rpi#1 tried to SSH onward with a key it doesn't have, failing in well under a second, silently swallowed by this function's own `|| true`; fixed by invoking `run_scenario.sh` directly on the runner, no extra SSH hop. (2) With that fixed, every scenario but one completed normally (~18-22s each, ~5 minutes total, matching the design estimate) - `liveliness_loss_detection` hung for the job's full 20-minute timeout and got cancelled; root cause, confirmed directly against both `client.c`'s and `server.c`'s own doc comments: that scenario's client never prints a `RESULT:` line and never exits on its own ("publishing... until killed" - departure has to be a real `kill -9`, not a graceful exit, matching the DDS twins' own scenario 8 design), so `run_scenario.sh`'s generic `ssh ... | grep '^RESULT:'` blocks forever waiting for a line that will never come - `server.c`'s own doc comment independently confirms this scenario "is invoked manually... since it needs a custom kill -9 step", never meant to go through the generic path; fixed with a dedicated `run_liveliness_scenario()` (starts server/client directly, waits 6s for real publishes to land, sends the real `pkill -9 -x client`, polls up to ~10s for the server's own RESULT line instead of blocking indefinitely, then SIGINTs the server as a safety net so a RESULT line lands either way). (3) `Check all` failed twice more on real, previously-undetected shellcheck findings (SC2034 an unused `sent` local, a copy-paste leftover; SC2086 a deliberately-unquoted `$client_args` word-splitting expression, whose disable directive first had to be repositioned since placing it between a `&&` continuation and its command confused shellcheck's own parser) - downloaded shellcheck v0.10.0 locally partway through (neither `shellcheck` nor `docker` was available in this environment) and ran it directly against every script under `.github/scripts/` before the final push, which should have been done from the very first push instead of relying on synthetic bash-level tests alone. **Real HIL confirmed working end-to-end, not just a clean exit code**: final push (`ae7b075`) completed in ~5 minutes with real, varied data across all 15 invocations (e.g. `reliable_throughput` recv 1492861/1443058/1417683 at 0/1/5% tc loss, 0.0/1.5/5.7% loss_pct; `liveliness_loss_detection` detect_latency_ms 1621/2626/3595 at lease=1.0/2.0/4.0s); the published `gh-pages` page independently re-cloned and inspected directly (not just trusted from the job's own "success" conclusion) - all 11 new `TickLE HIL - ...` categories present and populated in `dev/bench/data.js`, the status-grid's new 13-column Raspberry Pi section rendering correctly in `dev/bench/index.html`, the old 10 categories left untouched (frozen, not yet pruned - deferred to a later, separate `gh-pages`-only commit once the user/TickLE Plan confirm, per the plan's own explicit "not part of the main-branch PR" scoping). Rig usage coordinated with TickLE Plan before every push in this sequence, per this session's own established rig/CI discipline. | User's own benchmark-dashboard request (2026-09-22) | ✅ implemented, real CI green end-to-end (`ae7b075`), real HIL data confirmed on the live `gh-pages` page; old dashboard categories left in place pending a separate cleanup commit |

| **67** | ✅ **Same-host data delivery: every node now binds its own data port (TickLE Dev, commit `82a6a02d`; independently reviewed and re-verified by TickLE Plan).** The A1 piece of the three-way split (A1 per-node data port -> A2 interface-scoped broadcast -> A3 per-link unicast/broadcast decision) that TickLE Plan proposed and TickLE Dev refined, at the user's own approval ("1번 제안에 동의해. 2번 제안도 동의해. 진행하자"). **The defect**: every node bound only the well-known port with `SO_REUSEADDR`, so two nodes on one host shared an address and a unicast aimed at it was delivered by the kernel to exactly one of the two sockets - sometimes the sender's own. Discovery was unaffected (a broadcast reaches every socket bound to that port), so the pair matched and the graph looked healthy while the data went nowhere. **The fix**: each node also binds a kernel-assigned data port (`tt_hal.data_sock`) and sends everything from it, so every peer records this node at a port that is its alone. No wire-format change - `upsert_peer()` already learns a peer's port from the source address of the packet it was heard on. Discovery stays on the well-known port and broadcast, because a node must be reachable before anyone knows its data port. Both HAL backends (`hal_linux.c`, `hal_freertos.c`), since the hazard is about sharing a host, not about Linux. `tt_receive()` now polls on every path including the negative-timeout one, which previously read only the well-known socket. | 12 | ✅ landed, verified on both sides |

**TickLE Plan's own independent verification, not taken on report** (2026-09-23): read the whole `hal_linux.c`/`hal_linux.h` diff rather than the commit message; ran `make examples && make test-samehost` in this worktree from a clean rebase onto `82a6a02d` - `same-host test: PASS (5/5 delivered, publisher self-received 5 of 10 sent)`, matching TickLE Dev's own reported figures exactly. Confirmed the two claims that would have invalidated the rig: (a) `_tt_CONFIG.addr` is bound wildcard on both sockets, so the well-known socket still receives broadcast and the data socket receives unicast to any local address, and (b) **the HIL loss injection is unaffected by the new source port** - `run_perf.sh` applies `tc qdisc replace dev $CLIENT_IFACE root netem loss N%` to the interface's root qdisc with no `u32`/port filter anywhere in `examples/perf_hil` or `.github/scripts`, so no scenario's injected loss was ever keyed to the well-known port. That was checked before assuming it, because a source-port change is exactly the kind of thing that silently stops matching a filter.

**Two review findings TickLE Plan raised back to TickLE Dev, neither a blocker for A1:** (1) **`tt_receive()` prefers the well-known socket whenever both are ready**, and `tt_try_receive()` reads the data socket only on `EAGAIN` from the well-known one. Correct for delivery - `poll()` is level-triggered, so nothing is lost - but under *sustained* broadcast the data socket can be starved indefinitely rather than merely delayed. That matters specifically above `tt_UNICAST_PEER_THRESHOLD`, where data itself goes out as broadcast while ACKNACKs and retransmit requests come back on the data socket: the RELIABLE recovery path is then the thing being starved. It does not affect any measurement taken so far (the rig is two nodes, i.e. unicast, so data lands on the data socket), but it is the mode A3 is about to make the common one, so it wants a decision before A3 rather than after. (2) **`test_samehost.sh`'s second assertion is weaker than its own stated intent.** `pub_self -lt pub_tx` is satisfied by the exact benchmark signature the test exists to catch - 10009 self-received of 10010 sent passes it. The test still fails on the bug, but via the *delivery* assertion (`received -eq COUNT`), which is the symptom, not the mechanism. A bound of roughly `pub_self -le pub_tx - COUNT` ("the publisher must not have received back any of its own data samples") would pin the mechanism as the header claims; the exact arithmetic is TickLE Dev's call since it depends on `tt_Publisher_publish()`'s own batching. This is reasoned from the shell condition, not measured.

**Both findings fixed the same evening** (TickLE Dev, commit `2d72282c`), and re-verified here rather than taken on report. The two sockets now alternate whenever both are ready, in `tt_receive()` and `tt_try_receive()` alike (`tt_hal.rx_prefer_data`), which bounds the wait at one datagram in either direction instead of leaving a fixed preference that could starve the other socket outright. The same-host test's second assertion became `pub_tx - pub_self >= COUNT` - "at least `COUNT` of what the publisher sent did not come back" - with the derivation written into the script; TickLE Dev checked it against four cases (10010/10009 fails, 10/10 fails, 10/5 and 11/6 pass). TickLE Plan ran the test five times on a clean rebase onto `2d72282c`: 5/5 delivered every time, `tx`/`self` of 12/5 twice and 10/5 three times.

**One fragility that came out of those five runs, raised to TickLE Dev, not yet decided.** The new assertion is exact rather than generous: its margin is `pub_tx - pub_self - COUNT`, which was 0 in three of the five runs (10/5) and 2 in the other two (12/5, the extra two being announces that went out as unicast once the peer was known and so add to `tx` alone). Zero margin is fine while every data sample is unicast, which is what all five runs did. But `tt_UNICAST_PEER_THRESHOLD`'s own documented zero-known-peers fallback sends data as *broadcast* until a matching peer has been learned, and a broadcast data sample comes back to its own sender - adding one to `self` and one to `tx`, so the margin goes to -1 and the assertion fails on a system that is behaving correctly. Nothing observed in five runs (the subscriber gets a 2s head start, and discovery demonstrably completed before the first publish in every one), so this is reasoned reachability, not a measured flake. It matters only because this test is a gate on `test-all.yml` and runs on every push: the cost of a spurious failure there is paid by whoever pushes next, and a gate that can fail on correct behaviour teaches people to re-run it. Suggested to TickLE Dev either to make the publisher's first publish provably follow discovery, or to count data datagrams separately from announces so the assertion needs no margin at all.

**The fragility was real, and the run proved it rather than leaving it reasoned** (TickLE Dev, commit `fa3265f0`). The publisher's first sample does go out before any peer is known, so it is a broadcast, and a broadcast comes back to its own sender: `rx_self_sent_data` reads **1 on a completely healthy run**. That is exactly the mechanism predicted from `config.h`'s zero-known-peers rule, and it means the margin was not merely small but **already fully consumed** - the five passing runs were passing at the boundary, and a second such sample would have failed a gate that runs on every push. TickLE Dev took the second of the two suggested routes and ended the class rather than widening the margin: a new counter for self-received *unicast* data, which has no legitimate non-zero case at all (a node's own unicast is addressed to somebody else by construction), with the HAL recording which of the two sockets each datagram arrived on (`tt_Node.rx_via_data_port`, both backends). No arithmetic, no margin, no race. Re-verified here on a clean rebase, three runs: `5/5 delivered, publisher self-received 0 unicast data samples, 1 data samples in total`.

**A second defect found in the same reading, unrelated to the assertion**: `97657c0a`'s own traffic log in `tt_Node_destroy()` dereferenced `node` to print the counters *before* the function's NULL check, i.e. the guard ran after the thing it guards. Fixed in `fa3265f0`. TickLE Dev found it while repairing a text substitution of its own that had silently matched nothing (clang-format had reflowed the target line since the pattern was written), and drew the two together: a check that cannot fail is not a check, whether it is an assertion, a guard, or a text substitution. That is the same shape as this milestone's own process finding one level down, and is why both are recorded here rather than only in commit messages.

**A2's premise was wrong, and it was TickLE Plan's wording that was wrong** (found by TickLE Dev before writing any of A2, 2026-09-23). Both sessions had been describing A2 as "nothing binds the socket to a link, which is why the traffic leaked". The first half is true and the second does not follow: **a directed broadcast is already link-scoped by the routing table, with no socket binding involved at all.** Verified on this box rather than taken on report - `ip route get 10.1.1.255` and `ip route get 255.255.255.255` both resolve to `dev ens18 src 10.1.1.204`, and `ip route get 192.168.10.255` leaves via the gateway, i.e. the destination address alone selects the link. Re-run on both rpis directly and confirmed exactly: `192.168.10.255` -> `dev eth0 src 192.168.10.3` / `192.168.10.2`, `255.255.255.255` -> `dev wlan0 src 10.1.1.214` / `10.1.1.213`, with `default via 10.1.1.1 dev wlan0` on each. Two independent corroborations point the same way: `run_perf.sh` has always derived the tc interface with `ip route get $PERF_LINK_BROADCAST`, which only works if a directed broadcast is route-scoped, and the measured incident itself was `rmw_tickle` on the compiled-in default while the `perf_hil` harnesses (which set `192.168.10.255`) never appeared on `10.1.1.x`.

**So the defect is narrower than recorded, and the correction matters for what gets built**: `255.255.255.255` is the *limited* broadcast, which has no subnet to be scoped by and therefore follows whatever the default route happens to be - `wlan0` on the rpis, the lab network. It is the **default value** that is wrong, not the absence of a bind. Anyone who configures a directed broadcast is already scoped; anyone who does not is on the default route by definition, and no socket binding can recover which link they meant. A2 accordingly becomes three smaller pieces: **(1)** stop defaulting to `255.255.255.255` - refuse to start without an explicit link, or derive one from a configured bind address, rather than guessing; **(2)** scope the limited-broadcast case when it is deliberately chosen, where a bind *is* the right tool, noting that `SO_BINDTODEVICE` needs `CAP_NET_RAW` so the unprivileged form is binding the *data* socket to the chosen interface's own address, with the well-known socket left on `0.0.0.0` because a socket bound to a unicast address does not receive broadcasts (A1's socket split is what makes this possible at all); **(3)** say so in `config.h` beside `_tt_NODE_BROADCAST`, since "the default reaches further than you think" is not discoverable from the constant. **(1) is a behaviour change for anyone relying on the default and is therefore the user's decision, not ours** - it is also the piece that would have prevented the incident, and it is the direction the user already asked for ("가장 좋은건 사용자가 지정한 포트(인터페이스)로만 broadcast가 나가게 하는거야"). **(2) carries a claim that must be measured, not reasoned**: that binding a send socket to an interface's unicast address actually scopes a subsequent `255.255.255.255` send to that interface. That is plausible route-selection behaviour and it is exactly the kind of plausible thing this project has been wrong about twice today, so it needs a two-interface host and a packet capture before anything depends on it.

**A2 item 2's mechanism is now measured, not reasoned** (TickLE Plan, 2026-09-24, `examples/perf_hil/experiments/bind_scope_capture.sh`, rig at `e5916c98`). The claim was that binding the data socket to an interface's own address scopes a subsequent *limited* broadcast (`255.255.255.255`) to that interface. It cannot be asserted from inside the sender - the send succeeds either way and the sender cannot tell which link the kernel chose - so it is a negative that only a capture on the *other* interface can establish. rpi#1 is the one machine here with two links (`eth0` 192.168.10.3, the test link; `wlan0` 10.1.1.214, the lab LAN and the default route). Two arms, `tcpdump` on both interfaces filtered to `udp port 8282`, publisher sending 20 samples over ~2s, **three repetitions of each arm**:

| arm | publisher | eth0 | wlan0 |
|---|---|---|---|
| A (control, no bind address) | `-b 255.255.255.255` | 0, 0, 0 | 23, 22, 22 |
| B (data socket bound to eth0's address) | `-b 255.255.255.255 -a 192.168.10.3` | 22, 23, 22 | **0, 0, 0** |

**The mechanism holds, and the control proves the instrument could have seen a failure.** Arm A is the leak itself, reproduced deliberately and bounded: with nothing to scope it, every datagram left by `wlan0` onto the shared lab network and not one by the test link. Arm B moved all of it to `eth0` with nothing at all on `wlan0`, in all three repetitions. So `_tt_CONFIG.addr` on the data socket is a working unprivileged substitute for `SO_BINDTODEVICE`, and A2 item 2 may be relied on.

**A near-miss worth more than the result, because the instrument lied first.** The first completed run of this experiment reported `eth0=0 wlan0=0` in *both* arms - a reading that looks like "nothing leaves either interface", which would have been reported as a spectacular success for a fix that had not been exercised at all. What it actually meant was that the capture never started: `sudo -n timeout 12 tcpdump ...` asks `sudo` to run **`timeout`**, not `tcpdump`, and this host's NOPASSWD list has `tcpdump` and not `timeout` - and the script sent that failure to `/dev/null`. Two further false starts followed, each surfacing only because the previous fix stopped hiding errors: a readiness check that read an empty pcap as "did not start" (tcpdump buffers, so a running capture legitimately has a zero-byte file until `-U`), and then a genuine `Permission denied` from a root-owned pcap left by the previous attempt, which tcpdump could not overwrite after dropping privileges and which this host has no `sudo rm` to clear. What caught the first and worst of these was **the interpretation rule written into the script before it was ever run** - "eth0=0 in both arms means the publisher never sent, and neither arm says anything". Deciding in advance what a result would have to look like to mean something turned a convincing false negative into an obvious instrument failure. That is the cheapest safeguard found today and it belongs on every measurement this project runs.

**And a correction to how "detached" was being applied here.** This experiment was launched detached per CLAUDE.md's own rule 1, and was lost anyway when the session ended: the script and its output were both in the session scratchpad, which went with it. Detaching the *process* is only half of outliving a session - the script and the results file have to outlive it too. The experiment now lives in the repo and writes to `/tmp` outside any scratchpad.

**Same-host re-measurement done, and it clears the socket work** (TickLE Plan, 2026-09-24, three repetitions of `compare_rmw_perf.sh` at `f83721a0`, `COMPARISON.MD` §5 updated). Milestone 67 recorded that every same-host figure this project held had been taken through a transport misdelivering roughly a tenth of its datagrams; those cells are now replaced rather than annotated, since they measured a different system rather than this one imprecisely.

The first repetition looked like a regression - `rmw_tickle` at 57.0-103.6us against a pre-fix 46.4-54.3us - and was reported to TickLE Dev immediately, explicitly as n=1 and explicitly as not to be acted on. That was the right call twice over. The 103.6us cell did not recur (59.7 and 57.9 in the other two repetitions) and is recorded as an unexplained outlier rather than averaged in silently; and the apparent regression is not one. **Every implementation moved by about the same amount between the two measurement days**: FastDDS +12.4 to +19.5us, CycloneDDS +11.8 and +13.3us, `rmw_tickle` +5.7 to +15.1us, mean across all ten comparable cells roughly **+13us**. FastDDS and CycloneDDS contain no TickLE code, so a shift they share cannot have come from TickLE's. It is an additive box-level offset between the two days, cause not identified, and it means absolute latencies from the two passes are not comparable at all.

**The corollary is the part that would have been easy to get wrong in our own favour.** Within-pass, `rmw_tickle` now sits at 1.12-1.30x the DDS vendors where §5 previously read 1.3-1.6x, which looks like an improvement and is not one: a constant additive term compresses every ratio toward 1 by itself (adding 13us to 37.5 and 48.7 moves their ratio from 1.30 to 1.19 with nothing having changed). No improvement is claimed. What survives is narrower and true: within this pass the ratio is 1.12-1.30x, throughput is indistinguishable across all three, and **the wire-correctness defect cost correctness without a measurable latency cost in this particular benchmark**.

**For TickLE Dev's bisect note, now moot but worth keeping**: TickLE Dev pointed out that if a regression had survived repetition, `2d72282c` (the receive-socket alternation, up to 2N `recvfrom()` per drain instead of N) was at least as plausible a candidate as `82a6a02d` (the socket split), and that building `82a6a02d`'s receive path against HEAD isolates the alternation cheaply. It also proposed a cheaper alternation - flip the preference only after a read that produced data, keeping the starvation bound while not paying the wasted syscall when one socket carries everything. No number asks for that today, and it is recorded so the option exists rather than being rediscovered.

**Verification of the instrument, since two measurements lied earlier the same day**: the running benchmark was confirmed to have `/home/semih/tickle/install/rmw_tickle/lib/librmw_tickle.so` mapped, read out of `/proc/<pid>/maps` while it ran, rather than inferred from the build having succeeded. A stray reference to the CI runner's own install tree in an `rcutils` stale-error message was what prompted the check; it was not the library actually loaded. **Still outstanding**: §3b's Mbps cells and their provisional ◊ marks, which need the rig rather than this box.

**Every commit id in both documents was audited, and six of them were wrong** (TickLE Plan, 2026-09-24, prompted by TickLE Dev after `a437859` turned out not to exist). The §3b investigation stalled on a citation that named a commit rebased away before it was ever pushed, so the same check was run over every backtick-quoted sha in `PLAN.md` and `COMPARISON.MD`: resolve it, then assert it is an ancestor of `origin/main`. Six failed - `215e16c4` (cited in **both** documents, for the durability fix `COMPARISON.MD` §3b's own TRANSIENT_LOCAL result rests on), `a437859`, `7b266d2`, `83798dc`, `8f5ef5a` and `a365f4b`. All six had main-branch equivalents, found by matching the commit subject, and all are now corrected: `261f39b8`, `acaa8049`, `24c5b927`, `55da6b62` and `b7d822ab`. Note that `8f5ef5a` and `a365f4b` were two local iterations of one experiment that landed as the single commit `b7d822ab`, so the documents previously cited two shas for one change, neither of them real. <!-- doc-shas-ignore: this paragraph quotes the six broken shas as its subject, so the checker must not read them as citations -->

**Why this is worth more than the tidy-up it looks like.** Every one of these was written down at the moment the work was done, by whoever did it, citing the sha they had in front of them - and then a rebase before the push changed it. Nothing was careless; the local sha is simply not the published one, and no step in the workflow ever compared them. The cost landed hours later: an entire evening's investigation could not check whether a figure reproduced at its own commit, because the commit named did not exist. **The audit is two lines of shell** (`git cat-file -e` then `git merge-base --is-ancestor`) **and should run over these documents whenever either changes**, which is a better safeguard than remembering to re-check a sha after a rebase. Recording the sha with every published figure, agreed separately, does nothing on its own if the sha recorded is the pre-rebase one.

### The through-line of 2026-09-23/24: four times, a tool's output was indistinguishable from success

Recorded as one entry rather than scattered across the commits that happened to find each one, because the pattern is the finding and the four instances are only evidence for it. In each case something reported a pass, the report was read as information, and the thing being checked had not been checked at all.

1. **A capture that never started.** `sudo -n timeout 12 tcpdump ...` asks `sudo` to run **`timeout`**, which was not in the rig's NOPASSWD list, and the failure went to `/dev/null`. The experiment reported `eth0=0 wlan0=0` in both arms - which reads as "nothing leaves either interface", a triumphant result for a fix that had not been exercised.
2. **An assertion that could not fail.** `platform/linux/test_samehost.sh`'s second check was `pub_self -lt pub_tx`, which the exact benchmark signature it existed to catch passes comfortably (10009 self-received of 10010 sent). It named a mechanism in its comment and tested a symptom.
3. **A substitution that matched nothing.** A `sed` edit to the traffic log silently matched no lines, because `clang-format` had reflowed the target since the pattern was written - so a test ran against a binary without the field it asserted on, and reported the absence as a test bug.
4. **`make lint` reporting a pass for two different languages it never read.** It compiles no C++, so `rmw_tickle`'s `_Static_assert`-in-a-public-header break sat on a red `Check all` for eight commits while "lint is clean" was reported truthfully. Then, the same day, it reads no shell either, and a new script's `shellcheck` finding shipped the same way - on a machine where `shellcheck` was not installed, so there was nothing to notice.

**What unites them is not carelessness.** Every one produced output, that output was true, and in each case it was true about something other than the question being asked. A person reading it could not have distinguished it from the answer they wanted, because it *was* indistinguishable - no amount of care at the reading end recovers it.

**So the answers are all structural, and all four are now gates that were each verified to fail without the thing they check** - which is itself the lesson applied to its own fixes: `platform/linux/test_samehost.sh` (real sockets, two nodes, one host, every push), `make headers-cpp` (every public header compiled as C *and* C++), `make check-doc-shas` (every commit id these documents cite resolves and is reachable), `make lint-shell` (every tracked script, and it **refuses rather than skips** when `shellcheck` cannot be resolved, because a target that quietly passes over a whole language is not a weaker check but a false statement). Each prints what it checked, and `lint-shell` names the binary and version that answered - TickLE Plan spent an evening running `shellcheck` 0.10.0 from `/tmp` while CI ran a different version, and nothing said so.

**Three method rules earned the same way, with the cost each one already had:**

- **Write down what each outcome would have to look like to mean something, before running the measurement, and include the outcome that means "the instrument did not work".** This caught instance 1. The line "eth0=0 in both arms means the publisher never sent, and neither arm says anything" turned a convincing false negative into an obvious broken instrument.
- **But pre-registration does nothing about a false premise baked in beforehand, and makes it harder to see, because it now looks like rigour.** §3b's sweep was read as "the bimodality did not reproduce" when the harness pin excludes CPU0 by construction and the mode's precondition was absent from the whole sweep. The question was never "does placement vary" but "does it take the one value the mechanism is about". The premise - "the pin does not settle this" - was written into the script header and never checked; `/proc/interrupts` settles it in one line.
- **A pattern match is never evidence that a job is alive.** `pgrep -f compare_rmw_perf.sh` matched the checking command's own command line, and "the measurement is running" was reported to the user and to a peer session, and repeated for forty minutes, while the log had held the error the whole time. Now in `~/.claude/CLAUDE.md` rule 3, which previously covered only choosing a process to *kill*.

**The limit, stated because the four gates above could read as a solution.** None of them would have caught the pre-registration failure. A false premise written into a script *before* the run still looks like rigour afterwards - there is nothing for a gate to compare it against, because the script is internally consistent and its output is exactly what its own stated rule says to expect. Every one of the eight items on these two days was caught by the *other* session, and in each case the person who made it could not have seen it at the time: not through inattention, but because the thing that was wrong was the premise they were reasoning from. TickLE Dev could not have caught its own rebased sha; TickLE Plan could not have caught its own `taskset` premise; neither could have caught their own "`make lint` is clean". **So these four gates are not scaffolding that replaces the review - they are the four things the review happened to find, made durable.** The review is what finds the next four, and there is no automated substitute for a second reader holding a different premise.

**And one that is not about tools at all.** The same day's re-measurement produced a ratio that moved from 1.3-1.6x to 1.12-1.30x - which reads as `rmw_tickle` closing on two mature DDS stacks. It is an artifact: a constant additive offset compresses every ratio toward 1 by itself. It was the only number of the two days that pointed the way we would have wanted, and it is the one that most needed taking apart. `COMPARISON.MD` claims no improvement.

**The process finding, recorded because it outlives this bug.** The mechanism was not unknown to this project: `platform/linux/test.sh`'s own header has stated for some time that "a kernel delivers a *unicast* packet aimed at one wildcard-bound socket to whichever such socket bound last, not by any real address distinction, so that setup silently could not validate any of the unicast paths." The response at the time was to give *that test* two network namespaces so it would stop hitting the condition. The test passed from then on and the product stayed broken, and because the only suite that runs on every push mocks the HAL away entirely (`tests/test_mock.h`), nothing else was ever positioned to see it. It took a benchmark, months later, to find it. **The rule this yields**: when a test is changed so that it stops hitting a real condition, the condition is a product finding until something proves otherwise - the change closes the test's exposure to it, never the product's. `platform/linux/test_samehost.sh` is the standing answer here (real sockets, two real nodes, one host, over loopback; no root, no namespaces, so it runs on every push, wired into `make test-samehost`/`make test-all` and `test-all.yml`'s own gate). TickLE Dev raised this itself rather than leaving it in a commit message, which is the reason it is written down at this length.

**Consequence for every same-host number this project holds, restated because it is easy to under-count.** The failure was not confined to the runs that visibly delivered nothing. Roughly 10% of the stream went to the sender in runs that *passed and were recorded as clean*. That means every same-host `rmw_tickle` figure - `COMPARISON.MD` §5's latency table, the `compare_rmw_perf.sh` comparisons, and the CI benchmark history on `gh-pages` accumulated before `82a6a02d` - was taken through a transport that was quietly misdelivering a tenth of its traffic. Those numbers are not merely imprecise, they are measured through a different system than the one that exists now. TickLE Plan's own re-measurement obligation, to be run in one rig session once A2 lands: `COMPARISON.MD` §3b's Mbps cells for all three frameworks under `taskset -c 1-3` (lifting the provisional ◊ marks), and the §5 same-host latency table. The two-host HIL figures are unaffected - two hosts never shared a port - and are the reason the rig's own scenario results still stand.

## Build order

0 and 1 in parallel → 2 → 3 and 4 (parallel) and 5 (parallel, only needs 2) → 6 (only needs 0) →
7/8/9 alongside anything → 10 following each milestone as it lands, not saved for the end. 17
(TickLE core, not `rmw_tickle`) only needs 4 to already exist so its own bridge has something to
replace, and can proceed independently of 11/15/16's own C++/conformance track.
